‘The Invisible Guest’ Remake Director Danial Rifki on Suharto-Era Fear and Why His New Thriller Is About ‘How to Fight Back’

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Indonesian director Danial Rifki’s remake of Spanish thriller “The Invisible Guest,” which he says was completed in 2023 before exclusivity clauses with HBO held up its release, finally reached the big screen at Jakarta World Cinema.

It is one of three Rifki films at the festival.

The Falcon Pictures production, which reworks Oriol Paulo’s 2016 hit “Contratiempo” for an Indonesian setting, stars Reza Rahadian, Mawar de Jongh and Arifin Putra. It debuted on HBO Max on Aug. 7. Related Stories Murder-House Impostor Thriller 'The Stand-In Family' Wins Mirror Fiction Award at Taiwan Creative Content Fest 'Jimmy' Review: Cornball James Stewart Biopic Gets the Manner, but Not the Man

Rifki tells Variety that the matter has only recently been resolved.

He also has two new features at the festival: romantic comedy “Tua Tua Keladi” and thriller “Sandal Jepit No. 10.”

Set among the older residents of a small village, “Tua Tua Keladi” follows the chaos that breaks out when a chemical leak into the village water supply stirs up desire. Unspoken feelings, long-held confessions and passion come out overnight.

“I was thinking, if they have young energy to live the second life, [it is] the second chance to love,” Rifki says.

He wanted the film to challenge a deep-rooted assumption that older people no longer live with energy. For all the awkwardness of its premise, the film keeps away from eroticism, even with its younger characters.

In the digital era, Rifki says, people can record their sexual behavior openly – “and sometimes that kind of freaks me out because I have a daughter. I’m concerned if my daughter [is exposed to] wild media.”

“So with the young characters, I try to approach them with something pure, not physical,” he says.

“Sandal Jepit No. 10” is a confined-space thriller about the brutal police interrogation of Akbar, a grieving father detained on suspicion of murder.

“Indonesians have trauma in politics. Life back in the days of Orde Baru – the New Order in the Suharto era – for around 32 years, we could not criticize the government,” Rifki says. “Now we have the Reformation Era, but it might be happening underground. On social media, if you criticize the government, they will shadowban [your comment].”

“We are the low class of society with this kind of fear that, if we do something, someone with power will oppress us. So the movie is about how to fight back,” he says.

“If you have social media, netizens are here to help you out. People power now is turning [to] a digital era so we can gain that power again. I’m still sure that [there are] many good people,” he adds.

Rifki says the film grew out of his concern over abuse of power in Indonesia today.

“[When I was] a film student, I really wanted to try how to speak in rom-com, thriller, drama, and other genres. [I’m] an evolving and growing filmmaker still in the making,” he says.

The backbone of all his work, he says, is human moral conflict, whatever the genre. He is also keen to try action and horror.

His next film, “Before Thirty,” is set for release in January 2027. Rifki calls it a “survival drama” about a 29-year-old man whose life is failing.

“In Indonesia, people always ask: what is your achievement before 30? Young people are punished by that value that you have to be successful before the age of 30,” Rifki says.

The film, he says, is his “commentary on that issue.”

NYT Connections hints today: Clues, answers for November 8, 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The NYT Connections puzzle today is not too difficult if you're a gym rat.

Connections is the one of the most popular New York Times word games that's captured the public's attention. The game is all about finding the "common threads between words." And just like Wordle, Connections resets after midnight and each new set of words gets trickier and trickier—so we've served up some hints and tips to get you over the hurdle.

If you just want to be told today's puzzle, you can jump to the end of this article for today's Connections solution. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

The NYT's latest daily word game has become a social media hit. The Times credits associate puzzle editor Wyna Liu with helping to create the new word game and bringing it to the publications' Games section. Connections can be played on both web browsers and mobile devices and require players to group four words that share something in common.

Each puzzle features 16 words and each grouping of words is split into four categories. These sets could comprise of anything from book titles, software, country names, etc. Even though multiple words will seem like they fit together, there's only one correct answer.

If a player gets all four words in a set correct, those words are removed from the board. Guess wrong and it counts as a mistake—players get up to four mistakes until the game ends.

Players can also rearrange and shuffle the board to make spotting connections easier. Additionally, each group is color-coded with yellow being the easiest, followed by green, blue, and purple. Like Wordle, you can share the results with your friends on social media.

NYT Strands hints, answers for November 8, 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Today's NYT Strands hints will be easy if you can easily defend yourself.

Strands, the New York Times' elevated word-search game, requires the player to perform a twist on the classic word search. Words can be made from linked letters — up, down, left, right, or diagonal, but words can also change direction, resulting in quirky shapes and patterns. Every single letter in the grid will be part of an answer. There's always a theme linking every solution, along with the "spangram," a special, word or phrase that sums up that day's theme, and spans the entire grid horizontally or vertically.

By providing an opaque hint and not providing the word list, Strands creates a brain-teasing game that takes a little longer to play than its other games, like Wordle and Connections.

If you're feeling stuck or just don't have 10 or more minutes to figure out today's puzzle, we've got all the NYT Strands hints for today's puzzle you need to progress at your preferred pace.

Today's NYT Strands spangram is horizontal.

Wordle today: Answer, hints for November 8, 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Today's Wordle answer should be easy to solve if you're competitive.

If you just want to be told today's word, you can jump to the bottom of this article for today's Wordle solution revealed. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

Originally created by engineer Josh Wardle as a gift for his partner, Wordle rapidly spread to become an international phenomenon, with thousands of people around the globe playing every day. Alternate Wordle versions created by fans also sprang up, including battle royale Squabble, music identification game Heardle, and variations like Dordle and Quordle that make you guess multiple words at once.

Wordle eventually became so popular that it was purchased by the New York Times, and TikTok creators even livestream themselves playing.

The best Wordle starting word is the one that speaks to you. But if you prefer to be strategic in your approach, we have a few ideas to help you pick a word that might help you find the solution faster. One tip is to select a word that includes at least two different vowels, plus some common consonants like S, T, R, or N.

It might feel like Wordle is getting harder, but it actually isn't any more difficult than when it first began. You can turn on Wordle's Hard Mode if you're after more of a challenge, though.

Hurdle hints and answers for November 8, 2026

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

Weekly Rundown: Hims & Hers adds new partners for benefits program; Hippocratic unveils updates to healthcare-focused AI model" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Stay up to date on the latest in health tech, digital health and health AI news with this weekly brief. Read below for news from the week of Nov. 2 to 6.

Hims & Hers announced Thursday the addition of four new partners to its benefits program, bringing the total of included partners to 14.

The new partners are nutrition gummy maker Grüns; air medical transport membership service Medjet; AI-powered home strength system Tonal and full body intelligence provider Viome.

Superscript is opening access to its platform through the launch of a new API, which allows any healthcare practice or electronic health record system to access real-time healthcare pricing information.

The platform, backed by Mark Cuban and Nava Ventures, is currently live with 1,500 active healthcare providers.  To date, it has served more than 500,000 patients and processed 2.2 million patient billing transactions, executives say.

One of the organizations Superscript works with is Michigan-based Orthopaedic Associates of Muskegon (OAM).

“Patients deserve certainty not only in their clinical diagnosis and treatment plans, but also in their financial obligations,” said Jeff Cave, OAM CEO, in a statement. “Historically, doctors and administrative teams had no way of knowing exact insurance reimbursement rates at the point of care. Partnering with Superscript lets our patients know exactly what they owe before their visit. That transparency builds trust, eliminates anxiety, and allows our care team to focus entirely on delivering high-quality care.”

Hippocratic AI launched Monday a new generation of its healthcare language model that features six new upgrades to its AI agents.

The company says the new version of its healthcare-focused AI model can conduct more natural and responsive patient conversations by interpreting emotional cues, reasoning while speaking and adapting its voice in real time, while improving safety, speed and electronic health record system integration.

The upgrade—dubbed Polaris 6 Ultra—features what executives call “bedside intuition,” a first-of-its-kind capability that reads patients’ emotional state and decides next speech without pause.

“In healthcare, you don't get credit for reminding a patient. You get credit for the action they take,” said Munjal Shah, Hippocratic AI CEO and co-founder, in a statement. “Polaris 6.0 was built from more than 323 million patient conversations, making it the only truly experienced frontier model for healthcare able to operate safely and autonomously to drive patient outcomes. That experience shows. Polaris 6.0 handles the healthcare edge cases of real life, it now has more human-like common sense and pacing, and it delivers the kind of empathy that persuades patients.”

Other capabilities include AI intuition, an upgraded version of Care Voice and enhanced Common Sense.

Polaris 6 Ultra also runs on 7.4 trillion parameters and delivers a 1.3X end-to-end speedup compared to Polaris 5, the company says.

A coalition of organizations is calling on the U.S. Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA) to extend its telemedicine prescribing flexibilities deadline given the lack of finalization to a key rule.

The flexibilities are set to expire on Dec. 31, and the agency’s Special Registrations for Telemedicine and Limited State Telemedicine Registrations final rule has not been published.

The coalition said patients and providers are “facing unacceptable uncertainty and the real risk of care disruptions,” noting that providers and platforms will need time to review requirements and apply for registration even if the rule is published in November.

“Patients should not have to wonder in December whether their January appointments will happen—or whether they will lose access to medications they rely on,” said Chris Adamec, Alliance for Connected Care executive director, in a statement. “With 59 days remaining and no published final rule, DEA can give providers and patients the stability they need by issuing a fifth extension as soon as possible.”

Advocates are calling for an extension through at least Dec. 31, 2027.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/health-tech/hims-hers-inks-deal-eli-lilly-offer-branded-weight-loss-drugs" hreflang="en">Hims &amp; Hers inks deal with Eli Lilly to offer branded weight loss drugs</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Hims & Hers inks deal with Eli Lilly to offer branded weight loss drugs" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Hims & Hers struck a deal with Eli Lilly to offer the drugmaker’s branded obesity treatments through its online health and wellness platform.

Under the agreement, Eli Lilly will give Hims & Hers pharmacies direct access to its U.S. Food and Drug Administration-approved GLP-1 medications at self-pay prices. Eligible patients on the Hims & Hers platform can be prescribed and order Eli Lilly's injectable Zepbound (tirzepatide) and daily oral medication Foundayo (orforglipron) without leaving the company's platform.

Prior to this agreement, Lilly drugs were available to patients on Hims & Hers only if they left the site and went to Lilly's direct-to-consumer platform, LillyDirect.

Shares of Hims & Hers jumped 8% Friday following news of the Eli Lilly partnership.

The agreement brings together Lilly’s obesity medicines and Hims & Hers’ consumer platform to expand consumers' access to FDA-approved weight loss drugs, the companies said.

"Patients can complete a consultation, receive a prescription if their provider determines it is appropriate and have an FDA-approved Lilly medicine shipped directly to their home, all in one seamless experience," the companies said in a press release.

Self-pay pricing starts at $149 per month for Foundayo and $299 per month for Zepbound.

"At Lilly, we are always looking for new ways to help more people get the medicines they need, and that means meeting patients where they already are," said Ilya Yuffa, executive vice president and president, Lilly USA and global customer capabilities, Eli Lilly and Company, in a statement. "Millions of people turn to Hims & Hers for their care, and through this agreement, those patients now have a simple, more direct way to get FDA-approved Lilly medicine at an affordable price if prescribed."

Hims & Hers' technology platform supports provider outreach, follow-up care and treatment planning. Weight loss patients on Hims & Hers receive a membership experience that includes 24/7 access to a care team, nutrition guidance, regular clinical check-ins and access to the Hims & Hers Weight Loss community, according to the company.

The agreement with Eli Lilly mirrors Hims & Hers' partnership with Novo Nordisk, which has been selling its branded weight loss drugs Wegovy and Ozempic through Hims & Hers since March. Novo had previously sued the telehealth company for offering compounded versions of its medications. Hims & Hers' agreement with Novo marked a shift in its obesity strategy toward FDA-approved branded GLP-1 treatments and away from cheaper compounded GLP-1 drugs.

Earlier this year, Hims & Hers took heat from the pharmaceutical manufacturer and federal regulators after announcing plans to launch a knockoff of Novo's oral pill. It backed down from those plans under pressure.

Hims & Hers stated that it never offered access to compounded copies of Lilly’s GLP-1 drugs and has committed not to do so in the future.

As part of the agreement, the companies have committed to protecting independent clinical decision-making, so that providers on the Hims & Hers platform continue to prescribe the treatment they determine is right for each patient.

“Lilly is one of the most innovative pharmaceutical companies in history, and I’m incredibly proud that Hims & Hers is a part of making sure more people can access the medicines they’ve created,” said Andrew Dudum, co-founder and CEO of Hims & Hers, in a statement. “By working together, we’re not just reaching more people with obesity with seamless, personal access to FDA-approved Lilly medicine. We’re showing the industry what innovators can achieve for customers everywhere when we work together to make the world well.”

Hims & Hers has faced financial pressure as it shifts from high-margin compounded GLP-1s to lower-margin branded weight-loss drugs. The company posted a $92 million loss in Q1, including $33 million in restructuring costs, largely from write-downs of its compounded GLP-1 supply chain, which executives said faced “risk of obsolescence.”

In Q2, Hims & Hers reported an $86 million loss, compared with a $43 million profit a year earlier. The quarter included $81 million in one-time costs, including expenses tied to its exit from compounded GLP-1s.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/finance/omada-health-hikes-2026-outlook-after-strongest-quarter-ever-q3" hreflang="en">Omada Health hikes 2026 outlook after ‘strongest quarter ever’ in Q3</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Omada Health hikes 2026 outlook after ‘strongest quarter ever’ in Q3" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Omada Health saw its "strongest quarter ever" in the third quarter of 2026, CEO and co-founder Sean Duffy told investors and analysts during a Thursday evening earnings call.

"We delivered our highest quarterly revenue, gross margin progression, and our most profitable quarter to date," Duffy said.

The virtual chronic care provider, which went public last June, brought in $91 million in revenue—up 34% year-over-year—and reported gross margin of 76% on a GAAP basis and 78% on a non-GAAP basis, according to Duffy. Omada reported net income of $7 million in the third quarter, compared with a net loss of $3 million in Q3 2025.

The company also reported adjusted EBITDA of $12 million, compared to $2 million the year prior.

It beat Wall Street analysts’ estimates for revenue, though slightly trailed behind projections for total membership.

Total members reached 1.1 million, up 36% year-over-year, with trailing 12-month revenue per total member increasing 5% year-over-year to $294, according to financial results published Thursday. Average member tenure also grew 10% compared to the year prior, to approximately two years and four months. Member retention has improved to 58%, up from 43% in Q4 of 2024, executives said.

Omada Health President Wei-Li Shao said Q3 membership growth reflects "continued demand" for its cardiometabolic platform, adding that the company's diabetes and hypertension programs continued to lead growth.

The company's diabetes and hypertension programs, longer member retention and lower care delivery costs are increasingly driving both growth and profitability, executives said.

"Members stayed with us longer, and our ability to provide personalized care at scale reached a new level of efficiency," Shao said.

The company supports diabetes, hypertension, obesity and musculoskeletal conditions—launching a GLP-1 care companion solution last November.

Shao noted that Omada deepened its partnership with the Health Care Services Corporation (HCSC) last quarter to extend its prevention and hypertension programs into three additional states, which is expected to "potentially reach an additional estimated 1.5 million covered lives at launch in 2027."

"On the PBM side, we have commercial relationships with the three largest PBMs in the country, which together processed an estimated 80% of prescription claims in 2025," Shao said. "Our prescribing program is in the market now at Optum. These relationships give us the broadest distribution in Omada’s history for our programs."

Shao, who will take over as CEO Jan. 1, also noted that artificial intelligence solutions enable care teams to have more capacity to "apply their clinical expertise" while tailoring care to each member’s needs.

"This quarter shows what we have built at Omada," Shao said. "Our commercial relationships give us broad reach, and our cardiometabolic programs have driven growth. Our program mix has supported longer member tenure, and our cost to serve has decreased."

Chief Financial Officer Steve Cook told investors the company expects new enrollments to "moderate sequentially" in the fourth quarter, which he said is "consistent with historic seasonality."

Shao also noted that a number of employers have been spending more time making GLP-1 coverage and obesity care decisions, "given the size of the commitment, the range of solutions and the importance of getting these decisions right," which has extended the selling season deeper into the fourth quarter.

Omada Health's stock dropped 19% Friday following the Q3 earnings call on Thursday as investors pulled back due to concerns about the longer selling season and expected Q4 member decline clouding the company's 2027 outlook.

The company is raising its full 2026 revenue and adjusted EBITDA outlook. Cook said revenue is expected to range from $344 million to $346 million, up from its previous range of $334 million to $340 million. Adjusted EBITDA is expected to be in the range of $32 million to $34 million, up from $21 million to $27 million.

"Our outlook anticipates Q4 revenue below our Q3 level, reflecting the lower new enrollment and engagement activity we have historically seen during the holidays," Cook said. "We expect revenue to come primarily from continued engagement among existing members."

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/ai-and-machine-learning/equiti-acquires-lexi-ai-combine-ai-human-medical-interpretation" hreflang="en">Equiti acquires Lexi AI to combine AI, human medical interpretation</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Equiti acquires Lexi AI to combine AI, human medical interpretation" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Equiti is acquiring Lexi AI to build a hybrid language-access model that combines AI language support with human medical interpreters.

Equiti, which sells medical interpretation services to hospitals and health systems through its Martti business, provides 24/7 access to qualified medical interpreters across more than 250 languages and dialects. Lexi provides multilingual, AI-powered medical interpretation. The acquisition deal merges Lexi's immediate AI language support, covering over 35 languages and dialects, with Martti and its network of qualified medical interpreters to help healthcare organizations expand language access.

The acquisition was announced October 28. Financial details of the deal were not disclosed.

With this acquisition, Equiti is building on Martti’s work pioneering video remote interpretation in healthcare more than two decades ago, according to executives. The use of AI can expand where and when language services can be offered, particularly for everyday interactions that may not currently receive interpretation support, Equiti executives said.

Martti was built alongside healthcare organizations to make qualified interpreters available by video. Lexi, in turn, has been developed with healthcare customers to extend immediate language support into more workflows. The acquisition brings those capabilities together, with human-in-the-loop design, patient safety and patient experience at its center, Equiti executives said.

The company saw an opportunity to combine its services with Lexi's AI expertise rather than build a healthcare AI interpretation product internally, Maureen Huber, Equiti CEO, told Fierce Healthcare.

"Lexi brings specialized expertise in AI, engineering and healthcare product design, along with experience putting that technology into the hands of care teams. That combination takes time to build," Huber said. "For more than 20 years, our work through Martti has been about making qualified human interpretation accessible within healthcare. We understand the service, the clinical environment and what it takes to support healthcare organizations every day. Developing AI and machine learning technology calls for additional, specialized expertise. We wanted people who had already been doing that work with healthcare in mind."

She added, "We spent time evaluating the category and learning from customers. Lexi brought together the people, product and approach we were seeking. Combining their expertise with ours gives us a stronger foundation to expand language access responsibly."

Combining AI and human interpretation makes language support more accessible across the patient journey, helping organizations improve care delivery while making better use of their resources, according to Equiti executives.

Healthcare organizations are increasingly interested in AI interpretation capabilities and are looking for guidance, Huber noted.

"Customers were asking: 'Where could AI help? How do we evaluate it? What is appropriate for our patients?' Healthcare leaders see the same advances in consumer AI that everyone else sees. They also must think about what happens when those tools enter a care environment, sometimes before an organization has decided how they should be used. They want a partner who understands both the possibilities and the responsibilities," she said.

She added, "I would describe that as inquiry and evaluation. There is real curiosity about extending language support to more of the patient experience, particularly before and after a visit. Our job is to help customers identify those opportunities while keeping qualified human interpretation central to care delivery."

Equiti calls this approach Modality Matters, a connected model that allows healthcare organizations to match each encounter with the appropriate modality — immediate, healthcare-purpose-built AI support for approved use cases and qualified medical interpretation where clinical complexity, regulatory requirements, organizational policy or patient preference calls for a human interpreter. With this approach, organizations have the flexibility to shift between modalities as clinical context and communication needs change in real time.

The combined portfolio of Equiti and Lexi's capabilities will extend language access into everyday interactions that often go unsupported, Huber noted.

Equiti is focusing Lexi's AI-powered medical interpretation on routine patient engagement, including before and after care, such as appointment communications, scheduling and nonclinical follow-up logistics.

"We see a significant opportunity for Lexi in that communication before and after care: helping patients exchange messages, arrange appointments and stay connected in their preferred language. Language access must extend beyond the clinical encounter. Think about what happens after a patient leaves the clinic. They receive a message about a follow-up appointment, but they need to ask whether it is at the same location, how to reschedule, or whom to contact. Those may be simple questions for the organization, but the answers determine whether the patient can take the next step," she said.

Qualified human interpreters remain the foundation for care delivery, Huber noted. "In conversations such as those about a diagnosis, informed consent, treatment or palliative care, the ability to clarify meaning, recognize cultural context and respond with empathy is essential. A medically qualified interpreter helps people understand one another through conversations that can be complex and deeply personal," she said.

Equiti said it can work with customers to define appropriate uses within their policies and applicable requirements, preserving patient choice and access to a qualified human interpreter.

Language access is also a strategic performance driver for healthcare organizations, as effective communication influences patient understanding and experience, clinical outcomes, workforce efficiency and financial performance.

Equiti's customers include Wellstar Health System, Corewell Health, NYU Langone and Lee Health. Lexi works with a growing pipeline of federally qualified health centers and community providers including Lowell Community Health Center, which serves one of the most multilingual communities in Massachusetts.

As general-purpose AI gets better at translation, a healthcare-specific interpretation platform has distinct advantages, Huber asserted.

"Healthcare communication involves clinical meaning, cultural context, jargon and acronyms during situations where people may be frightened, vulnerable or unsure how to ask a question. Our job is to remove those communication barriers, and that requires understanding the care environment, fitting into clinical workflows and knowing when a qualified human interpreter needs to be involved," she said. "That is where Equiti’s experience matters. We bring the relationships, service infrastructure and human expertise developed through more than two decades of healthcare interpretation, alongside Lexi’s technology and team."

Huber said she envisions broader uses of Lexi's AI language support in healthcare workflows. "A patient’s need to communicate continues after the conversation with a clinician ends. Lexi already supports text-based messaging, including translation of text provided by a care team, and can even leave voicemail messages for patients. That creates opportunities to help patients access information and communicate between visits. Helping someone navigate follow-up care is a natural direction for that work, like understanding the next step, connecting with the right office or arranging an appointment. The value is in making it easier for patients to act on the care plan they have been given and ask for help along the way," she said.

"We built Lexi to fit the way healthcare teams actually work, bringing immediate language support into the devices and workflows they already use,” Siddharth UR, co-founder and chief product officer of Lexi, said in a statement. “As part of Equiti, we can extend that experience across the patient journey and connect it to a broader language access model built around the needs of each encounter.”

Lexi will operate alongside Martti under Equiti, with the teams working together on implementation and integration across healthcare workflows. Equiti will work with each customer to determine how the solutions fit within its language access program, clinical environment, and organizational policies.

« D’habitude, j’ai beaucoup à dire pour défendre notre équipe, mais pas ce soir » : Ruben Amorim accable ses joueurs après le fiasco de l'AC Milan

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Quatre jours après le succès dans le derby milanais, l'AC Milan a sombré à domicile face à Ferencváros en Ligue Europa. Excédé par le visage affiché par son équipe, l'entraîneur portugais Ruben Amorim a recadré ses joueurs en conférence de presse d'après-match.

Le jeudi 5 novembre 2026, San Siro a été le théâtre d'une immense désillusion pour les Rossoneri, battus (0-2) par la formation hongroise lors de la 4e journée de la phase de ligue. Une prestation indigne des ambitions lombardes qui place l'AC Milan dans une situation délicate sur la scène européenne.

Face aux journalistes à l'issue du coup de sifflet final, le technicien portugais n'a pas mâché ses mots au moment d'analyser la copie rendue par ses hommes. Ruben Amorim a notamment qualifié la première période d'« affreuse », refusant catégoriquement de chercher la moindre circonstance atténuante à ce défaite devant le public milanais. Le ton est donné.

Pourtant galvanisé par sa prestigieuse victoire face à l'Inter (3-1) en Serie A le week-end précédent, le club lombard a déçu. Ruben Amorim avait procédé à une large rotation dans son onze de départ, où seuls Ramos, Rabiot, Musah, De Winter et Diawara avaient débuté le choc face aux Nerazzurri. Les Milanais ont craqué au retour des vestiaires sur une grave erreur de contrôle du gardien de 21 ans Lorenzo Torriani, aligné pour suppléer Mike Maignan suspendu, sur une passe en retrait du défenseur Koni De Winter sous la pression de Kristoffer Zachariassen. L'attaquant haïtien Lenny Joseph en a profité pour ouvrir le score à la 52e minute, avant de signer un doublé à la 66e minute.

L'expulsion directe de Yusuf Bamidele à la 77e minute pour un coup de coude sur Mario Gila a laissé Ferencváros en infériorité numérique pour le dernier quart d'heure, sans que Milan ne parvienne à réduire l'écart. Les entrées en jeu en cours de rencontre de Diego Moreira, Christian Pulisic et Luka Modric n'ont pas permis aux Milanais d'inverser le cours du match. En zone mixte après la rencontre, Koni De Winter a d'ailleurs partagé le constat général : « Ce sont encore ces moments de perte de concentration. Aujourd’hui (hier), rien n’allait du tout. Je ne peux pas l’expliquer. Tout est une question de mental : la façon dont nous abordons les matchs, la façon dont nous jouons. C’est notre point faible ».

Au-delà de la défaite, la situation comptable devient particulièrement alarmante pour les Milanais, qui pointent désormais à une inquiétante 28e place au classement général de la Ligue Europa avec seulement 3 points en quatre rencontres, soit une victoire et trois défaites. Pour tenter de redresser la barre, Ruben Amorim a insisté sur le manque de culture de travail et a érigé Strahinja Pavlović en modèle d'engagement, estimant que tout le monde devrait s'entraîner comme lui.

L'AC Milan tentera de réagir en Serie A lors de son prochain déplacement sur la pelouse du Genoa, programmé le dimanche 8 novembre 2026 à 15h00 au stade Luigi Ferraris.

Mercato - Ça bouge à l’OM : Qui doit être le prochain joueur à partir cet été ?

Set up model-radar: catch free LLMs that really make tool calls

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

model-radar is two small commands I run for my AI agents:

Neither asks a model for an opinion. They measure and act. Setup is about ten minutes, and the guide is run on a fresh Ubuntu machine every week.

Python 3.11 or newer, nothing to pip install.

Before adding a key, run both with no credentials:

That exit 1 matters. If the canary can't check anything, a timer should notice, not shrug.

One line per model: PASS, FAIL or BUSY. BUSY means rate-limited or timed out, and it's never treated as a verdict, it just gets tried next run. On 8 October that gave me 4 passes out of 8 probed, two 403s and two busy. One free model I'd tried earlier answered every request and never once called the tool.

Run it once without --dry-run to save a baseline. From then on it only probes models it hasn't seen.

Point it at whatever your agents use (a JSON file, or hermes-agent profiles), add a paid fallback ladder and a local model in config.toml, and run it every few hours from cron. Two hard failures in a row and it walks the ladder. Timeouts and 429s never count.

Anything with a key. The weekly run has none, so it proves install, config, help and the no-key behaviour. The probes and the canary's failover need your OpenRouter account.

Full guide: github.com/casareanderson/model-radar/blob/main/docs/SETUP.md

How do you pick models for your agents, and how do you find out when one quietly stops working?

Security Risks of AI-Generated Code: Research and a Review Checklist

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at shipfastdev.com

AI-generated code usually works, but it is often not secure. In Veracode's 2026 tests, models produced code that compiled almost 100% of the time, yet failed security checks on nearly 44% of tasks, a pass rate that has not improved since 2025. The fix is not to ban AI tools; it is to treat their output like code from a contractor you have never worked with: review it, scan it, and test it before it reaches production.

Below is a summary of the research, the failure patterns that come up again and again, and a review checklist you can hand to your developer or agency.

In its 2025 GenAI Code Security Report, Veracode gave 80 curated coding tasks to more than 100 large language models. When a secure and an insecure approach were both possible, models chose the insecure one 45% of the time. Java was the riskiest language, with a security failure rate above 70%; Python, C# and JavaScript failed between 38% and 45% of the time. Models failed to defend against cross-site scripting in 86% of cases and log injection in 88%. Larger models did not perform significantly better than smaller ones.

The 2026 edition found the average security pass rate "sits at 56 percent — virtually unchanged since last year's report." The best model on its summer 2026 leaderboard reached 68%, which still means failing nearly one security task in three. Python code passed 63% of the time, Java only 30%. Coding-specialised models (51%) did no better than general-purpose ones (52%), and model size made no clear difference, although reasoning models kept an edge (56% against 51%).

Google's 2025 DORA report found that AI adoption is linked to higher delivery throughput but still has a negative relationship with delivery stability. Its explanation applies directly to security: without strong automated testing and fast feedback loops, more change volume leads to more incidents.

Security usually depends on context the model does not have: which input comes from an untrusted user, who is allowed to see which record, which secrets exist in your environment. Cross-site scripting, which depends on knowing where a piece of data will be displayed, had one of the worst results in Veracode's 2025 tests (86% failure). The model writes code that does what you asked; whether it is safe depends on what you did not say.

You don't need to read code to use this list. Ask your developer to confirm each point for every feature that touches users, payments or data. Items 1 to 4 apply before any change is merged, 5 to 7 cover dependencies and secrets, and 8 to 12 cover testing and process.

These questions belong in any evaluation of a development partner. If your app was built mainly with an AI app builder, read where vibe-coded MVPs break as well: missing data-access rules are the most common problem there.

Not by default. Veracode's 2026 tests found an average security pass rate of 56%, unchanged from 2025, even though almost all generated code compiled. It needs the same review and testing as any other code, and arguably more.

In Veracode's tests, Java: a 30% security pass rate in 2026, against 63% for Python. Language matters less than review, though: every language failed a significant share of tasks.

It can help spot issues, but the same models chose insecure options in a large share of Veracode's tasks. Use AI review as one layer, alongside static analysis, dependency audits, tests and a human reviewer.

If it handles personal data, payments or logins, at least run through the checklist above before launch. A full external audit makes sense once you have real users or sensitive data at scale.

A Backdoor Sat in the Firmware Partition of 1.3 Million Set-Top Boxes

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at blog.neticslabs.com

Capture of the Google Cloud Threat Intelligence post “Shutting the Door on the XCPE Botnet”, taken 3 November 2026 from cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/shutting-the-door-on-the-xcpe-botnet.

On 2 November 2026 Google Threat Intelligence Group published Shutting the Door on the XCPE Botnet, with the disclosure that Google and industry partners had dismantled a backdoor enrolled on more than 1.3 million set-top boxes. The attribution is the part that changes how the story should be read: the researchers assess with high confidence that the malware was developed, deployed and operated for financial gain by Videostrong Technology, an Android original equipment and original design manufacturer.

The malicious software arrived preinstalled, across many device models and brands, built directly into firmware over multiple years. Google found dedicated infrastructure, evolving configurations and tools for managing affiliates, and assessed with high confidence that Videostrong intentionally developed, deployed and operated the backdoor and botnet for financial gain. Its response was to cut ties with Videostrong and ban the company from making devices with preloaded Google apps.

Google's own account of how the code got past certification is worth reading twice. Certified Android devices pass a suite of compatibility and security tests, and the vendor used obfuscation to exploit a gap in those tests, loading malicious code after the device's first boot. Review processes changed as a result. For anyone who buys devices on the strength of a certification mark, this is the uncomfortable lesson: certification covers the tested state, and the vendor retains control over code loaded after first boot.

Netics editorial visual: the scale Google reported for the enrolled device population.

Three stages are documented, and the first one explains why removal fails. A stub package, shipped as x.cpe in some builds and com.oemservice in others, sits in the system partition and runs at android.uid.system privilege. On boot it checks the oem/ directory, installs the corresponding x.cpe package into /data/app/ and reinstalls itself after any user uninstall, which makes the device's own settings screen an unreliable place to look for the truth.

Stage two offloads the work. The backdoor acts mainly as the command-and-control communicator and passes module execution to com.abc.nexus, a file that carries native anti-analysis utilities to defeat sandbox detection and configures the monetisation modules. Since August 2026 that loader has been phased out in favour of tailored droppers that install a single module, delivered through the same command channel. Stage three is the modules themselves, arriving as native ELF daemons, as SDKs compiled into the loader's bytecode, or as secondary APKs fetched remotely. Google says a significant majority of the staging files—modules, shell scripts and legitimate applications—were hosted as GitHub releases on operator-owned GitHub pages.

Google Threat Intelligence Group figure, 2 November 2026: the XCPE ecosystem, from the infected set-top box and its backdoor through staging infrastructure to command servers and modules. Source: cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/shutting-the-door-on-the-xcpe-botnet.

The same bootstrap returns geolocation and STUN parameters so the device can find its public address and traverse NAT, a file-upload URL for logs and diagnostic dumps, and switches for individual advertising modules. All samples analysed to that point used a single broker hostname, mqtt.szbboys[.]com. Command traffic that looks like ordinary telemetry and a broker reachable over the public internet describe a detection problem that lives in network logs, where most small organisations look last.

The economic design is a rented shop window. Videostrong sold backdoor access to criminal affiliates, and the loaded modules turn the box into a residential proxy exit node, an ad-fraud engine, or a host for further backdoors. The named SDKs are specific: Linkfog, AppSalt and NetNut among the proxy networks, CBOXTV and HiddenView among the ad-fraud engines, Moyu and Spirit among the backdoors that load more modules. AppSalt is the residential proxy SDK owned by Honeygain, which is owned by Oxylabs, a detail that shows how ordinary the corporate chains behind these modules look.

Greed shows in the stacking. A single device can run several modules with the same function, and the researchers report as many as three residential proxy SDKs installed on one device. A compromised box becomes a metered egress point for someone else's traffic, with the household or office IP address carrying the reputation damage.

Netics editorial visual: the three-stage chain from firmware stub to monetisation module, and the step that ended it.

The partner list behind the disruption reads like a cross-section of the industry: Japan's National Police Agency, Lumen, HUMAN Security, Plume, Synthient and TrendAI.

This story turns firmware provenance into an inventory problem. A new image can fix a kernel or container flaw on every host that rebuilds. This backdoor was compiled into the firmware partition of devices already deployed in living rooms; the vendor is barred from certification and the hardware remains in the field.

Google's disruption made three moves worth noting for any operator. It took legal action against the domains and servers used to control the botnet and to install modules. It took legal action against command domains used by more than a dozen affiliate malware families, which the researchers assess severs access to at least 10 million additional compromised devices, since those affiliates distribute through other channels too. And it changed how firmware integrity is verified on certified Android TV devices.

Devices keep reporting while the broker answers, so the distribution below shows where the installed base sits.

Google Threat Intelligence Group figure, 2 November 2026: the global distribution of devices reporting the x.cpe backdoor. Source: cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/shutting-the-door-on-the-xcpe-botnet.

The operator's version of that work starts with a list. Which Android boxes, signage players, conference-room dongles and kiosk displays are on the network, who bought them, which vendor built the firmware and whether that vendor still exists in a position to ship updates. Most small estates cannot answer the last question, and that gap is the exposure this campaign exploits.

Google cut off command infrastructure and tightened firmware verification. Google took legal action to take down the domains, affiliate command servers are down, a manufacturer is out of the certified programme, and Google says it tightened firmware verification. A box already installed retains its backdoor. The researchers are explicit that the illicit market is resilient, with operators pivoting to new loaders or rival SDKs when a component is disrupted.

For a small organisation, the practical controls are unglamorous and cheap. Segment devices that need outbound internet from the rest of the network, deny egress to hosts nobody recognises, and watch for long-lived sessions to message brokers, which almost no television box needs. Prefer hardware whose firmware updates you can verify, and treat a bargain Android box from an unknown ODM as an untrusted endpoint with a screen attached. Prebuilt images carry their own provenance and inventory requirements. The earlier analysis of the Copy Fail container flaw argued from the software side; a full security audit and hardening is where an estate turns that reasoning into a list of devices it can defend.

“Backdoor operators, ad-fraud rings, and residential proxy providers operate as an interconnected supply chain.” The sentence closes the report, and it also describes the buying decision in front of any organisation refreshing its screens this quarter. The supply chain runs through the firmware partition, and the receipt is the only document most buyers keep.

Netics editorial visual: the report's closing framing, quoted from Google Threat Intelligence Group.

Need sovereign AI infrastructure, cloud cost audits, or automation pipelines? We build and ship them.

The Sky Today on Friday, November 6: Nab the North America Nebula

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for a sky event this week? Check out our full Sky This Week column.

As fall gets into full swing, we are seeing less of the summertime stars. By 6 P.M. local time, the Summer Triangle — which flies high overhead on short summer nights — is now starting to set in the west. Lowest of its three points is Vega in Lyra; to Vega’s upper left is Altair in Aquila, while Deneb in Cygnus is almost directly above Vega and the highest of the three.

With no Moon in the sky, let’s use Deneb to find a favorite deep-sky object: NGC 7000, the North America Nebula. It’s a great small scope target, and its location just 3° east of Deneb makes it easy to center in the eyepiece. However, this huge emission nebula stretches about 2° across, so note that it requires a larger field of view to capture all in one go. It definitely benefits from a nebula filter if you have one.

On the sky, the nebula’s western side looks like the eastern coast of the continent for which it is named, and vice versa. The curve that mimics the Gulf of Mexico is particularly prominent, so start there if you’re having trouble seeing the shape. Look also to NGC 7000’s west — across a dark gulf, you’ll see a fainter, more compact patch of nebulosity. This is IC 5067/5070, also known as the Pelican Nebula.

Sunrise: 6:35 A.M.Sunset: 4:52 P.M.Moonrise: 3:56 A.M.Moonset: 3:16 P.M.Moon Phase: Waning crescent (5%)*Times for sunrise, sunset, moonrise, and moonset are given in local time from 40° N 90° W. The Moon’s illumination is given at 10 P.M. local time from the same location.

Alison Klesman is senior editor of Astronomy magazine. She holds a Ph.D. in astronomy and has studied a variety of topics, from minor planets to supermassive black holes.

Stop Stale Editors Overwriting Changes with If-Match

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Two people open the same settings page. One changes the title; the other saves a description a minute later. Both receive a successful response, but the second save restores the old title.

The second person did not deliberately undo anything. Their editor submitted an old copy of the document. If your API accepts every replacement unconditionally, the most recent request wins even when it contains older information.

For an HTTP API, a useful defence is a strong ETag paired with If-Match. The client sends the version it edited; the server applies the change only if that version is still current. A mismatch normally produces 412 Precondition Failed, giving the editor a chance to reconcile instead of silently overwriting.

Keep the quoted validator with the loaded document. When saving, send it unchanged:

If another editor has already produced version 18, the condition is false. RFC 9110's If-Match rules define this conditional request behaviour. The header uses strong comparison: a weak tag such as W/"settings-17" cannot satisfy it. See Mozilla's If-Match reference.

Treat the ETag as an opaque value. Clients should not increment its number or reconstruct it from a timestamp. The server owns its meaning, including which representation it identifies.

This small model demonstrates the save rule. Save it as conditional-save.mjs and run node conditional-save.mjs with a current Node.js release. It uses only the built-in assertion module:

Ben's description is still in his local editor. It was never accepted by the server. That is the point at which the interface should offer a comparison, not announce that everything has been saved.

A server implementation can still lose updates if it checks the version, yields to another request, and later writes without a condition. Comparing the tag in one database query and performing an unconditional update in another recreates the race.

For a versioned row, the core operation might be:

Interpret the affected-row count and distinguish a missing resource from a stale version according to your API contract. Authenticate and authorise the caller independently. For an ETag representing several rows, the transaction must cover every change contributing to that representation.

This SQL is an implementation sketch, not a complete HTTP validator parser. A production endpoint must also handle required-header policy, validation, representation variants and its database's transaction behaviour.

On 412, keep the unsaved changes, fetch the latest document and show what changed. Automatically replacing the ETag and resubmitting the old whole document defeats the protection. A field-by-field merge can help, but overlapping edits still need a deliberate decision.

If-Match: * is not equivalent to a version-specific tag: it checks for an existing representation, not whether the editor saw the current one.

Also separate concurrency from transport uncertainty. If a response disappears after a successful save, the caller needs to discover the outcome; retry design is a separate concern. Jitsumi's explanation of API idempotency and uncertain retries works through that boundary.

For caching, the related validator question is usually whether a stored representation can be reused. Jitsumi's ETag and Cache-Control example covers that read path. The save path here asks a different question: is this still the version the editor is entitled to replace?

In Zagreb, the company that owns the robotaxis chose the aggregator on day one

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On 26 March 2026 Uber, Verne and Pony.ai announced a three-way deal to bring robotaxis to Europe. Pony.ai supplies the autonomous driving, Croatian company Verne buys the cars and keeps them on the road, Uber brings the network. That same announcement already stated that rides would be bookable "via Uber as well as through Verne's app", and that Uber "intends to invest an undisclosed amount into Verne".

Two weeks later, on 8 April, Europe's first commercial robotaxi service started in Zagreb: Verne's app, about ninety square kilometres of the city including the airport, from seven in the morning to nine at night.

On 19 August the rides also appeared on Uber's app, with a licensed operator on board behind the wheel, Verne acting as fleet owner and service operator. On 10 September the first rides with nobody at the wheel began, on the twenty-two kilometres to the airport: test rides for invited passengers, while the commercial service kept the operator on board. On 7 October the investment was confirmed by Verne's chief executive, Marko Pejković: "I can confirm, yes, Uber has invested in Verne." The size of the stake was not disclosed.

The press covered the machine. For anyone coordinating a fleet, the thing to look at is different, and it is not an ambush: it is a choice. Europe's most advanced operator, the one that actually owns the driverless cars and has its own app, decided from day one to also go through the aggregator's app, and to let the aggregator into its capital.

If that is what the company with the robotaxis does, it is worth asking what a dispatch company will do: one with the shifts, the licences and thirty years of customers, but without a list to restart from.

You do not need to wait for robotaxis to see how this ends. It has already happened twice, with human drivers.

August 2020: Uber buys Autocab, the British company that supplies booking and dispatch software to private hire operators, which is to say to its own competitors. On 29 March 2021 the UK competition authority closed its inquiry finding "no competition concerns", and the deal completed in April.

It is worth reading why it closed. The authority had considered exactly the data question, whether operators might be pushed into handing data to Uber, and set it aside by observing that there are "other credible suppliers" those operators "could switch" to if the service worsened or if data were demanded. In other words: in the authority's reasoning, the protection is not a promise, it is being able to leave.

The operators' main question at the time was the same one: what happens to our data. Autocab answered that it would handle them as always, without sharing. No abuse has emerged since, and that is not the point. The point is that the answer was a statement and not a clause, while the real guarantee had already been named by the regulator: the ability to walk away.

April 2025: Lyft buys FreeNow for 175 million euros. It was not buying cars. Ninety per cent of the gross bookings on that platform in 2024 came from taxis, which is to say from other people's vehicles and licences.

In both cases whoever was driving stayed at the wheel. What changed is who stands on the other side: in the first the supplier became the competitor, in the second the app changed owner.

In Italy the instinctive answer is that none of this applies yet, since the road code defines a vehicle as a machine "driven by a human" and a car that drives itself does not fit. Testing is nonetheless governed by the 2018 Smart Road decree.

But those rules defend the licence, not the relationship with the person getting into the car. No law obliges a passenger to call one number rather than another. Customers go where booking is easier, and they do not ask anyone's permission.

Original (Italian): A Zagabria anche l'operatore locale passa da Uber

Disclosure: I build UrbanGo, dispatch software for licensed taxi and private-hire operators. Everything above is sourced; this paragraph is the part where I sell something.

UK Warns Its Critical Infrastructure Faces Grave Risk

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

Britain's new Labour-led government is making a concerted push to get ahead of the AI risks to its national networks and the industrial complex.

At various levels of the United Kingdom government, officials are warning about the threat of cyberattacks against its critical infrastructure.

His Majesty's Government broadcasted its cybersecurity agenda on Nov. 3, releasing a variety of policy papers, an annual report from its lead cybersecurity agency, and related announcements pertaining to the security of small and medium-sized businesses (SMBs), supply chain vendors, critical national infrastructure (CNI), and more. These items all followed from an updated Autumn Budget 2026, released last week, which provisioned nearly two hundred billion pounds for AI-driven government cybersecurity projects.

Amid all of the practical policy updates, the headline quote of the day came from the state's National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), which assessed that it's "highly likely that between now and 2028, AI‑powered malware will have been used in a ransomware attack on UK CNI." It also labeled "highly likely" the prospect that "China, Russia, and other nation states will routinely use AI cyber to achieve their geopolitical goals" in the country.

Related:Third Serengeti Sweep Tops 1,400 Arrests as Investigations Mature

In a new report, Manchester-based NCC Group polled Members of Parliament (MPs) about the state of the UK's CNI cybersecurity posture. About half (51%) of MPs reported that their areas of the country are "not keeping pace" with the threats to its infrastructure. Only 22% expressed confidence in their country's posture. Asked about the nation's ability to fight off a major attack against its CNI, 47% believe that it's unprepared, whereas 41% feel it is.

The survey data is best read as aspirational, not prescriptive. There's little evidence that the UK is less prepared than other nations in addressing threats to its critical infrastructure, or that politicians would be able to tell one way or the other. Leaders are simply aware of the magnitude of what faces them.

"The challenge isn't a lack of government action, it’s proof that action translates into resilience," says Katharina Sommer, head of government affairs at NCC Group. "There's still no tested blueprint for coordinating cyber response effectively across government and industry, and that’s not a problem unique to the UK. The test for critical infrastructure operators is whether they can demonstrate, before an attack hits, that they can sustain essential services, recover quickly and maintain public confidence," which remains an open question.

A clearer message came through on Tuesday, from the experts at the NCSC. Using a scale in which "likely" accords with roughly 55 to 75% odds, "highly likely" with 80% to 90% odds, and "almost certain" approaching 100%, it bet that it is:

Related:UAE Transitions 50% of Government Services to Agentic AI

Likely that threat actors will use AI cyber tools to needle vulnerabilities in CNI

Highly likely that nation-states will use AI to increase the speed and sophistication of their cyberattacks, in service of their geopolitical goals in the UK; in particular, the agency identified China as the foremost player in terms of intent and capability to hit CNI and other strategic targets

Almost certain that "many systems will remain vulnerable" to AI-empowered attacks

The NCSC also drew out some future scenarios. Optimistically, it acknowledged that AI-driven cyber threat development might slow, allowing a window of time for the UK to catch up with the problem. Pessimistically, it painted a picture in which AI threats totally outpace the UK's ability to keep up.

"The situation is likely to be worse than is currently known, with many variables and unknowns still to be discovered," according to the report.

The message out of the UK government on Tuesday was, more or less: we're trying to get ahead of this.

In a policy paper, the government acknowledged that 2018's Network and Information Systems (NIS) Regulations — the UK's only multisector cybersecurity law — is "out of date and insufficient to tackle the threats we face." In a speech announcing its Annual Review 2026, NCSC CEO Richard Horne called the law built to replace NIS 2018 — November 2025's Cyber Security and Resilience Bill — "a vital step forward." The updated bill brings data centers, managed service providers (MSPs), and specific "critical suppliers" into the fold, and introduces stricter breach reporting requirements and hefty fines for negligence.

Related:Australian Gov't Weighs Mandatory AI Incident Reporting

In a separate policy paper concerning the same bill, the government stated its intention to introduce a new vendor risk management framework focused specifically on the "national security risks posed by vendors" connected to essential service providers. And in yet a third, related paper, the government described how the Cyber Security and Resilience Bill will be used to account for agentic "loss of control" incidents, in the fashion of this summer's AI industry lab leaks. Projected future measures include:

Security and resilience requirements to prevent loss of control incidents

Empowering ministers to perform postmortems

The bill is currently passing through Parliament.

Besides this one bill, the UK government also announced £2.4 billion in subsidies to help micro-, small, and medium-sized businesses attain "Cyber Essentials" certifications. All of this week's announcements come on the heels of an Autumn Budget last week, which arranged for tens of billions of pounds each to fund, among other things:

A National Exposure and Real-Time Vulnerability (NERV) Centre, for vulnerability discovery across the country, including CNI

A National Cyber Shield, for training AI agents to automate cyber defense

A separate National Agentic Security Unit for fighting rogue AI agents

"Increasing the funding and focus on cybersecurity to help keep the UK secure is very welcome," says Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence for KnowBe4. At the same time, he adds, "the main challenge I see is scale."

"£154 million for cyber-defenses is a good step," he believes, "but the NCSC estimates cyberattacks cost UK organizations £14.7 billion a year."

Dark Reading asked cybersecurity experts what governments like the UK's need to do to prevent future worst case scenarios. They consistently emphasized the same point:

"AI did not create new weaknesses, it revealed them. Most incidents still start through the same familiar routes, such as phishing, stolen credentials and the traditional kill chain paths attackers have relied on for years. The key to stopping these threats is, surprisingly, not exciting: it's the fundamentals," Chapman argues.

The fundamentals for Graham Taylor CBE, director of defense strategy for Northern Europe at OPSWAT, starts with protecting the edge. "Too many critical sites rely on firewalls and VPNs for connections that only need to send data one way. Where a facility only exports monitoring data, a hardware-enforced data diode removes the return path entirely, so neither a human attacker nor AI can use that link to send commands into the facility," he says.

Jacob Krell, Suzu Labs senior director for secure AI solutions and cybersecurity, emphasizes network segmentation "built like a boat." AI is going to make the perimeter easier to breach, he says, "and it cuts the time to objective once an attacker is inside. Critical infrastructure is notoriously vulnerable here because operators are hesitant to patch and test systems, and their caution is rational, a bad update takes a plant offline. So the perimeter fails more often and the interior offers nothing to slow lateral movement."

John Strand, owner of Black Hills Information Security, tosses out a less conventional suggestion for fighting off AI threats to CNI. "If AI gives attackers the ability to do a tremendous amount of work very quickly, defenders have an opportunity to present them with large numbers of decoys to investigate. We need creative ways to make those decoys look realistic and dynamic. The more time attacking AI agents spend interacting with decoys, the less time they spend attacking real systems," he argues.

Nate Nelson is a journalist and award-winning scriptwriter. In addition to Dark Reading he writes for Darknet Diaries, the most popular show in cybersecurity across all media.

He began his career as a freelancer, ghostwriting Forbes and CNBC op-eds for executives in tech and finance. Then he transitioned to journalism at Threatpost, where he covered cybersecurity news and trends. Throughout those years he co-created a cybersecurity podcast, Malicious Life, which in its day climbed into the Top 20 technology podcasts charts on Apple Podcasts and Spotify.

He holds degrees from New York University and Bard College. As a born and bred New Yorker, he enjoys a superiority complex, but is polite enough to keep it to himself.
