InfoQ Homepage Articles Identity as an Architectural Concern: Enterprise IAM Beyond Authentication

Nov 06, 2026 12 min read

For a single application, identity is mostly a vendor decision. You pick an identity provider, wire up SSO, validate tokens at the edge, and move on with the features. For one application and one team, that's the right call. It stops being enough as the organization grows. Login keeps working fine. Everything after that gets difficult: who approved a given access and whether it should still exist when an auditor asks about it months later.

This article draws on building and operating an identity and access management platform in regulated financial services, where a wrong entitlement becomes an audit finding. My argument is that at enterprise scale, identity has to be treated as part of the architecture. Teams that add it late tend to find it conflicts with decisions they've already locked in. I'll assume you have experience shipping OAuth 2.0 or SSO. What follows covers the design problems that appear once many services, and many owners, are involved.

Identity belongs in architecture discussions because it constrains decisions that look unrelated. The data model is where this shows up first. Once you have to explain and audit access, "who can touch this resource" needs to be a relationship you can query with history, because auditors ask what was true six months ago as well as what is true today. Service boundaries and event flows raise their own problems; each gets its own section below.

Explore how Azul helps Java teams reduce JVM vulnerability risk, strengthen application security, and prepare their Java environments for agentic AI. Discover practical strategies for securing Java workloads while maintaining performance and reliability. Learn more.

Regulation adds another constraint. Here's a useful test: Could you answer "Who had access to this system on 14 March?" from your data model alone, without grepping logs across several tools? If not, an audit wasn't part of the design.

A security review can catch a missing check or a misconfigured scope, but it can't retrofit a schema that was never built to answer that question. The cheapest way to pass that test is to record every grant, revocation, and attestation as an event with a timestamp and an actor from the start. Then the auditor's question is one query against the event log.

The Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) model is used by default and is usually sufficient. It strains when your questions shift from "Does this user have this role?" to how things relate to each other. Our entities are people, the service accounts they own, and the accesses those accounts hold. The questions we kept asking were: "Who owns this account?", "Is this access still valid?", and "What does this person control?"

All three are relationship questions. Ownership is a relationship. An access has a validity window and an attestation history attached to it. A person who leaves or changes roles pulls a set of owned accounts along behind them. In a role table, you rebuild each of those links with joins every time you ask. In a graph, each link is an edge and the query walks them.

That's why our backend models these relationships in Neo4j, queried through GraphQL. GraphQL fits because a query's nesting mirrors the traversal: this account, then its owner, and then its access. A caller asks for exactly the slice of the graph it needs, from an account down to its valid accesses. Nobody handwrites traversal logic or over-fetches a large object and filters it in memory. Figure 1 shows the shape of the underlying model.

Figure 1. Ownership-and-access graph modeled in Neo4j, showing how flat RBAC breaks down when access needs expiration and nuance. (Image source: created by the author.)

Before the query, here is the model it runs against. There are three kinds of nodes: Person, ServiceAccount, and Resource. There are two associations between them. First, a person OWNS a service account, while second, a service account HAS_ACCESS to a resource. The association, HAS_ACCESS, possesses some properties such as expiration, the date for when the access will be no longer valid, and lastAttested, which displays the last date when the access was attested by the owner. Validity is an attribute of the edge rather than of any node since it refers to the particular combination of the account and resource nodes.

This query, as shown below, poses a question we frequently ask ourselves: Who is the owner of this account and which of its accesses are currently valid? Looking through the query from the top, the first MATCH gets the account based on its ID and follows the OWNS relationship until it reaches the owner. The next OPTIONAL MATCH follows the HAS_ACCESS relationship towards each of the resources, and the WHERE clause appended to it filters out any access where the expiry date has expired. Because it is an OPTIONAL MATCH, an account having no accesses left will yield its owner anyway. Finally, the RETURN clause yields the owner’s name along with all the accesses left with their resources, expiry dates, and last attested date in a single array.

Written as SQL against role tables, the same question needs every link lined up by hand:

Even here, it's easy to get it wrong. Put the expiry check in the WHERE clause instead of the join, and accounts with no valid grants vanish from the result, owner and all. The query works, but it still leaves three problems out of view. Ownership is a single join in this example, but ownership can be delegated, and once it can, that join becomes a walk up a chain of unknown length. In the graph, the same chain is a variable-length path in one pattern. The expiry check also has to be repeated in every query that touches grants. Any query that leaves it out returns access that should have been removed. In the graph, the check sits inside the traversal, so a consumer can't skip it by accident. Finally, when a person leaves, the accounts they own aren't in front of you, and you have to go looking for them. In the graph they're one hop away, because "owns" is an edge. Both queries are simplified from what we run in production.

But Neo4j has inherent costs. It's one more datastore to operate, and traversal performance depends heavily on how you model things. A badly shaped graph can be slower than the join it replaced. It was still worth it for us, because ownership and validity are things we traverse constantly. The flat version pushes the hard part onto whoever writes the next query.

Comparing everything in the platform, the service accounts took the most governance work. Every service account is owned by a real person: Roughly eighty thousand people review, rotate, and vault the accounts they own. An account’s safety depends mostly on that owner: whether they're still engaged and whether they've changed teams or left the company. An account whose owner has moved on is exactly the stale, over-privileged credential that nobody notices until it's misused.

Before the rebuild, the three things an owner had to do (attestation, rotation, and vaulting) lived in three separate tools. An owner had to remember which tool did what. That's a small annoyance for someone who does it daily. For someone who does it twice a year, or who inherited an account when a colleague left, it's a real failure mode.

The rebuild put all three owner requirements into a single workflow, so an owner sees one screen with what they own and what's due. Getting there took more than wiring screens together. Each tool had been built with its own assumptions about schedules and ownership, and once they shared a workflow, those assumptions had to be reconciled. Figure 2 shows the revocation workflow before and after the consolidation: Three separate tools, with revocation taking about three days, became one unified workflow that revokes in under a day.

Figure 2. Revocation workflow before and after tool consolidation. (Image source: created by author.)

In enterprise identity work, the risky integrations are usually the ones where your system depends on a system you don't govern. For us, that was vaulting. We already handled attestation and rotation well before the rebuild. We had written that code ourselves, so we knew how it failed. Vaulting ran in a separate tool, owned by another team, on infrastructure we couldn't change. Folding it into our workflow meant a critical control now depended on their release schedule and their outages.

The hard part was deciding what our workflow should believe when their API didn't answer, or answered slowly, or answered after our call had already timed out. Those are not edge cases when you're doing this for tens of thousands of owners. They happen every day, somewhere. So we made the integration "fail closed". If the vault was unreachable, the workflow didn't report the step as complete, and an owner was never told a credential was vaulted when it wasn't. That sounds obvious on paper. In practice it meant our side couldn't treat "we sent the request" as success. We had to confirm the vault's own state, reconcile it against ours, and treat any gap between the two as something our side resolves.

If every service makes its own access decisions, authorization logic ends up scattered across dozens of codebases, where nobody can audit it. When a rule changes, you hunt for it through every service that has implemented its own version, and you can miss one. What works is letting identity context travel across boundaries while the decision stays in a dedicated policy decision point. Identity propagates through scoped claims using OAuth 2.0 token exchange, so a service acting for a caller gets a narrowed token instead of reusing a broad one.

The controller asks, and the policy decision point decides as shown in the following code snippet:

The controller never sees the rules, so changing a rule requires changing the PDP and nothing else.

Across a service hop, the incoming token is exchanged for a scoped one before the downstream call:

The token for each hop records its audience and scope, so the audit log shows what every call was for.

Entitlement changes propagate as events using Kafka. A grant, a revocation, or a change of ownership becomes a message that interested services consume. That's a sensible design for a distributed system and it indicates revocation is eventually consistent. Access stops once consumers process the event and invalidate any cached decisions, which happens some time after the revocation is recorded. That gap should be measured and capped before anyone describes revocation to a security stakeholder.

A consumer handling a revocation invalidates the cached decision, so the next check re-resolves from the source of truth:

Events can be lost, so the cache also has a time limit. A cached decision is served only while it's younger than a maximum age. After that, it's re-resolved from the source of truth whether or not an event arrived. That maximum age is the worst case scenario for a dropped event. Before we relied on the design, I simulated the consumer and cache to confirm that a missed event never kept access live past it.

Choosing the maximum age involves a real trade-off. A short maximum age causes stale access to expire quickly, which is what security wants, but every expiry sends another check back to the source of truth. At our volume that load isn't free. A long maximum age is easy on the system and worse for revocation, because a dropped event has longer to matter. You pick a number you can live with on both sides, then state it plainly: A lost event leaves access live for at most that long. That's a sentence a security team can plan around.

The cache window was the small part, though. The larger delay was procedural. Before the rebuild, fully revoking access took around three days, most of the time being spent on steps a person had to complete in separate tools. With a single workflow, access revocation takes under a day.

For a single app with a few roles and one team, you can skip all of this. Flat RBAC in your primary database is the right choice, and Neo4j, Kafka, and a policy layer would be over-engineering you'll regret maintaining. The threshold is organizational: many services making access decisions, service accounts that outnumber and outlive the people minding them, ownership that has to be re-verified, and an audit obligation with teeth. Here's how I would weigh each piece:

Authorization is moving out of individual services and into policy engines that can be tested on their own. For service accounts, the zero-trust version of this is continuous verification: an account re-proves its entitlements on a schedule instead of keeping them by default. Our service account rebuild was a manual version of the same idea, because attestation is reverification with a human in the loop. The interesting question is how much of that human step can be made automatic without losing the accountability that a named owner provides.

InfoQ Homepage News Vault Fork OpenBao Adds External Keys and Quantum-Ready TLS

Nov 06, 2026 4 min read

OpenBao has released version 2.7, adding read scaling for PostgreSQL-backed deployments, support for external hardware security module (HSM) and key management service (KMS) keys, and post-quantum cryptographic algorithms. The release also introduces control groups for human review of sensitive operations and new headers intended to keep requests consistent across load-balanced OpenBao nodes.

OpenBao began as a community fork of HashiCorp Vault after HashiCorp changed Vault's licence from the Mozilla Public License to the Business Source License in 2023. The project is now governed under the Linux Foundation's Open Source Security Foundation and is distributed under MPL 2.0, an Open Source Initiative-approved licence. Its stated aim is to provide a secrets manager under open governance rather than control by a single vendor.

The fork was both ideological and commercial. Some users objected to the loss of an OSI-approved licence and to the possibility that a vendor could change the terms governing infrastructure they had already adopted. Others saw a practical opportunity: Vault had placed capabilities such as namespaces, HSM integration and advanced scalability behind enterprise licences, while an open fork could make comparable functionality available without those restrictions. The move followed the wider pattern that produced OpenTofu from Terraform and Valkey from Redis.

OpenBao has developed well beyond a simple continuation of Vault Community Edition. Since the fork, the project has added namespaces for multi-tenant isolation, plugin-based auto-unseal integrations, and read scalability for its integrated Raft storage backend. Version 2.7 extends that scaling work to PostgreSQL, allowing standby OpenBao instances using that backend to serve reads in a similar way to Raft-based deployments.

Alex Scheel announced the release for the OpenBao project, writing that it adds "PostgreSQL horizontal scalability, External HSM and KMS-Backed Keys support, and Post-Quantum Cryptography". He also noted that the release arrived after a shorter development cycle than the previous one and included "many substantial features and several major security patches".

The external keys feature lets the PKI and Transit secrets engines use key material held in an HSM or KMS rather than storing it inside OpenBao. Support is delivered through third-party plugins, building on the auto-unseal plugin work in the previous release. This should appeal to organisations that must keep root certification authority keys or other high-value material under hardware control.

The post-quantum work adds ML-DSA and ML-KEM to TLS listeners, certificate authentication, PKI and Transit. Operators can therefore use post-quantum signatures and key exchange in relevant parts of the platform. The change does not by itself make a deployment quantum-safe: clients, certificate authorities and surrounding systems must also support the chosen algorithms. It does, however, give OpenBao users a route to begin testing hybrid and post-quantum configurations.

Control groups add an approval workflow to OpenBao. A new control_group stanza in an ACL policy can require a second party to review access to specified paths. This is aimed at high-risk operations where a single authenticated identity should not be able to act alone.

The release also includes breaking changes. The separate HSM distribution has been discontinued because the PKCS#11 plugin is now externalised. Vendor-specific built-in auto-unseal mechanisms for Alibaba Cloud, AWS, Azure, Google Cloud and Oracle Cloud have also moved to plugins. Kerberos, LDAP and RADIUS authentication, along with the LDAP secrets engine, have moved into the openbao-plugins repository. The file storage backend has been removed; the project had never recommended it for production because it lacks high availability and transactions.

Luca Berton, who interviewed Scheel at Open Source Summit Europe, wrote that external keys allow operators to "run your PKI root backed by an HSM" with key material comparable to Vault Enterprise managed keys. He also highlighted the importance of post-quantum support before government transitions expected around 2030, citing the risk that encrypted traffic recorded today could be decrypted in the future.

Commercial support is also becoming part of the OpenBao ecosystem. ControlPlane launched ControlPlane Enterprise for OpenBao in March, offering security hardening, supply-chain assurance, rapid CVE remediation, optional FIPS 140-3 compliance support and 24/7 technical support. That offering illustrates how an open-source project can remain freely licensed while vendors compete on operational assurance. ControlPlane positions the product as a supported path for organisations that want OpenBao's licence and governance model but still need enterprise-style operational commitments. Its materials also contrast OpenBao's MPL 2.0 licence with Vault's BSL 1.1 licence and note that OpenBao retains API compatibility with Vault for many use cases.

Secrets management being a foundational dependency has been covered in InfoQ recently. We previously reported on Vault 2.0 and IBM's support model following its acquisition of HashiCorp, as well as on the broader pattern of community forks preserving open standards after vendor licence changes. OpenBao's progress suggests that the fork is becoming a credible alternative rather than merely a protest project.

OpenBao version 2.8 is expected to focus more on security and stability, with likely improvements to authentication. For teams evaluating a migration, the immediate questions are less about individual features than about operational maturity: plugin lifecycle management, PostgreSQL high-availability configuration, post-quantum client compatibility and the availability of support for production incidents.

Find the bugs your tests miss. Antithesis uses deterministic simulation to explore thousands of failure scenarios, uncovering hard-to-reproduce bugs before they reach production. See how it works.

InfoQ Homepage News GitHub Actions Lockfiles Reach GA to Secure Workflow Dependencies

Nov 06, 2026 3 min read

GitHub has made workflow dependency lockfiles for GitHub Actions generally available on October 28, 2026, two weeks after the feature entered public preview. Workflows can maintain clear tag and branch references. The runner executes the specific commits listed in the .github/workflows/actions.lock file, even if those references change later. The feature is available on GitHub Enterprise Cloud, Enterprise Cloud with data residency, Team, and Free plans. The GA release includes the capabilities from the preview.

The change targets a long-standing weakness in how Actions resolves dependencies. A reference like actions/checkout@v4 or a branch name can change. This means a hacked action can add bad code to a pipeline without altering the repository using it. Teams can pin to full commit SHAs, but this reduces readability. It also leaves transitive dependencies, like those from composite actions, unpinned. Lockfiles address both gaps by recording commits for direct and transitive dependencies.

Tooling comes through the gh actions-lock extension for the GitHub CLI, hosted in the gh-actions-lock repository, which generates and maintains the lockfile. Adoption follows the workflow. Once a workflow is onboarded, every dependency needs a lockfile entry. The Actions runtime then runs the recorded commit, no matter where the tag or branch points. This lets teams migrate incrementally rather than converting an entire organization at once. The extension has a verification mode. It checks if the lockfile is up to date without changing any files. This way, it can act as a CI gate to spot differences between workflow files and the lockfile.

Dependabot integration addresses the usual cost of pinning, which is stale pins. When Dependabot opens a version update for an action in an onboarded workflow, it also updates the matching lockfile entry. Both changes appear in the same pull request, so the workflow reference and locked commit stay in sync. GitHub says this works without additional configuration. For governance, enterprise, organization, and repository admins can enable a "Require lockfile" policy. This policy helps enforce adoption at the relevant scope.

GitHub is explicit about the limits of the mechanism. A lockfile guarantees that runs use the commits you locked, but it does not determine whether those commits are safe, and the announcement advises reviewing dependencies before locking them. The security value therefore depends on the review that happens when an entry is created or updated. In practice, the Dependabot pull request becomes the review point, and teams that auto-merge those updates will carry any malicious change straight into the lockfile.

The release arrives as GitHub tightens other Actions defaults. A November 2 changelog entry announced that pull_request_target will be blocked by default starting December 2, another change aimed at limiting how untrusted code reaches privileged workflow contexts.

For teams evaluating adoption, setup instructions are in the GitHub Docs page on workflow dependency lockfiles. A reasonable starting point is to onboard a few high-privilege workflows, such as release or deployment pipelines, add the staleness check to CI, and confirm that Dependabot's combined pull requests fit existing review practices before enabling the enterprise-wide policy.

Competing platforms have not announced a native equivalent so far. GitLab's pipeline security guidance advises users to manually pin includes and components to commit SHAs. Users should also pin images to digests. After the March 2026 supply chain incidents, GitLab introduced open-source pipeline execution policies. These policies focus on blocking known attack patterns instead of creating a dependency manifest. Microsoft's Zero Trust guidance for Azure Pipelines also suggests using SHA pinning. It advises limiting Marketplace extensions too.

CircleCI orbs use a central semantic-versioning registry instead of commit hashes. This approach keeps the registry immutable. Teams on these platforms often use cross-platform tools. For example, they might use pinner to pin tags to SHAs and add a CI verification step. They may also rely on scanners like ActionSieve to flag unpinned references.

These tools pin the reference written in the pipeline file but do not resolve transitive dependencies, which is the gap GitHub's lockfile design addresses. As with GitHub's feature, none of them judges whether a pinned dependency is trustworthy, so the review step that precedes locking remains the control that matters.

The Biggest Stock Winners and Losers From the Midterm Elections

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The market historically performs well after midterm elections, but some stocks appear to be clear winners and losers following the results.

The midterm elections shifted the political landscape in the U.S., and, regardless of anyone's politics, that will impact some industries and stocks in a meaningful way going forward. Stocks have historically performed well following the midterm elections, regardless of which party wins. The S&P 500 (^GSPC -4.36%) has climbed from November to November 95% of the time since 1938, so now should still be a good time to buy stocks in general.

However, let's look at some stocks set to benefit from the election results and others that could be hurt.

Tariffs have been a bane to the retail sector, and the election results give retailers hope that there could be a future shift in policy. This isn't likely to happen right away and may not take hold until after the 2028 elections. However, the market tends to be forward-looking and already anticipating better days for retailers. Here are three retail-oriented stocks that could benefit.

Amazon (AMZN -2.19%) is the largest e-commerce company in both the U.S. and the world. The company's logistics network gives it a significant advantage, and it has been seeing strong operating leverage from its investments in AI and robotics. Meanwhile, any removal of tariffs should be a big boost to its retail operations, as it would increase consumer demand and help expand margins on the goods it sells.

Interestingly, Amazon's AWS unit is also viewed as a winner. While the elections were seen as a backlash against the AI data center build-out, this is expected to hurt neoclouds and benefit the big cloud providers. The reason is that the big cloud computing companies are generally better positioned to navigate regulatory hurdles, given their established relationships with utilities, regulators, and local governments, as well as their better financial resources and ability to secure long-term power agreements.

Shopify (SHOP -6.06%) has become the e-commerce software company retailers are increasingly turning to in an effort to take on Amazon. The stronger its retail customers are and the better they are performing, the more it benefits Shopify, which gets a revenue cut through its Shopify Payments solution.

The company also appears to be one of the biggest potential AI winners. This is a top stock to own if tariff pressures ease and consumer spending strengthens.

e.l.f. Beauty (ELF -3.33%) was one of the hardest-hit brands from tariffs, given most of its products are manufactured in China. Meanwhile, a $1 price increase for its namesake brand impacted its sales growth. As such, a rollback in tariffs could be a huge benefit to the company.

Meanwhile, the company's acquisition of Hailey Bieber's Rhode brand is proving to be a huge winner. The company is expanding Rhode's product assortment and distribution, which is leading to strong growth. A tariff-reduction tailwind would only add to its growth story.

Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -9.05%) has become one of the largest neoclouds, with aggressive goals to expand its computing power capacity. Tougher permitting could put a real dent in its ability to grow as quickly as it would like, with permitting delays leading to lost revenue. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa, for instance, specifically ran on limiting data center growth.

Meanwhile, it could also hamper SpaceX's ability to launch another U.S. mobile provider and complicate a potential merger with Tesla.

On the upside, if SpaceX can overcome technical hurdles and costs come down, its orbital data center ambitions could become a real advantage in a tougher terrestrial data center permitting environment. Unfortunately, the same can't be said for other neoclouds like CoreWeave (CRWV -9.83%), Iren (IREN -16.95%), and Nebius (NBIS -14.95%).

Palantir Technologies (PLTR -9.73%) has become one of the most important AI software-as-a-service (SaaS) companies on the planet, with its solution akin to an AI operating system that makes AI more useful in the real world. Its growth has been outstanding, driven by U.S. commercial customers, but the U.S. government remains its largest client.

The company's U.S. government growth has been strong, but a potential shift in administration could slow it. Given the stock's hefty valuation, any slowdown in growth could hurt the stock. However, I'd likely view any significant dip as a buying opportunity.

Geoffrey Seiler has positions in Amazon and e.l.f. Beauty. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon, Palantir Technologies, and Shopify. The Motley Fool recommends Space Exploration Technologies and e.l.f. Beauty. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

*Average returns of all recommendations since inception. Cost basis and return based on previous market day close.

Tanks or drones? Defense earnings reveal a more complicated rearmament boom

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Drone technology and artificial intelligence are rapidly reshaping modern warfare, but the world's biggest defense contractors are finding that demand for tanks, fighter jets and other traditional military equipment remains strong.

Recent earnings from U.S. and European defense companies show conventional weapons continue to drive year-on-year growth, even alongside surging demand for advanced sensors, autonomous systems and counter-drone technology.

Rather than displacing traditional weapons manufacturers, the rise of drones and autonomous warfare is creating new business for many of the same companies.

From counter-drone defenses to advanced sensors integrated into tanks and fighter jets, the technologies transforming the battlefield are also changing, or even expanding, the markets served by legacy defense giants.

Lockheed Martin reported a 24% jump in revenue from its missiles business in the third quarter, while RTX secured multibillion-dollar contracts for established missile systems. In Europe, German defense supplier Renk saw orders for its military vehicle business surge 46% in the first nine months of the year.

Yet, the same earnings also show how the boundaries between legacy weapons and emerging defense technology are increasingly blurred.

German defense electronics company Hensoldt reported a 52% jump in revenue from its optronics business, with its chief financial officer telling CNBC that much of the growth came from conventional military equipment, including tanks and armored vehicles.

Meanwhile, Rheinmetall's air defense business, which includes counter-drone systems, reported 63% revenue growth, even as the wider company cut its full-year outlook, citing project delays.

The rise of drones is transforming traditional military platforms rather than replacing them, with tanks and other weapons increasingly integrated into connected, data-driven battlefield networks, according to Morningstar.

Over the past five years and since Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in early 2022, defense primes have seen their order books swell amid higher military spending among governments globally.

Yet, analysts mostly say that the rearmament boom is structural, and even an end to wars in Ukraine and Iran won't derail it. Crucially, Europe now has to play catch-up amid increasing uncertainty over U.S. security guarantees and Russia's next move, after decades of military underfunding.

However, concerns that valuations had run ahead of the industry's ability to deliver dented defense stocks this year amid several instances of project delays and contract cancellations.

Advancements in modern warfare and technology – as proven by Ukraine's use of drones on the front lines – further hit investor confidence in defense contractors amid worries that new technologies may displace demand for traditional military equipment.

Beyond the front line, NATO plans to invest more than $40 billion in counter-drone capabilities over the next five years, as Secretary General Mark Rutte warned that drones have fundamentally transformed modern warfare.

Even so, Hensoldt CFO Christian Ladurner told CNBC on Thursday that demand for traditional military equipment remains a key driver of growth.

Its fast-growing optronics business, which supplies optical and sensor technologies, is benefiting from European governments' efforts to replenish conventional military equipment, even as newer battlefield technologies attract increasing attention, Ladurner said.

He also highlighted growing demand for air defense systems, as militaries confront an expanding range of threats, from small drones to ballistic missiles, adding that the air defense market is "underinvested" despite growing by 20% a year.

Limits to Patriot interceptors produced by Lockheed Martin are being felt hard, not least in Ukraine, after the U.S. depleted much of its stockpile of the state-of-the-art system able to take down high-speed missiles in the Iran war.

More recently, defense stocks have come under pressure amid concerns that the Democrats' U.S. midterm victories could squeeze American military spending.

While the result has dampened expectations for defense spending, with Democratic control of Congress making a proposed $1.5 trillion budget unlikely and potentially weighing on military spending growth through 2029, nothing has materially changed the geopolitical calculations, TD Cowen analyst Roman Schweizer told CNBC's "Morning Call" on Thursday.

"This is a multi-year picture, and I think we've seen those changes both in the U.S. and also Europe," he said, referencing internal budgetary difficulties and questions around NATO's hiked spending targets.

Morningstar analyst Loredana Muharremi told CNBC Wednesday's weakness in European defense stocks was largely sentiment-driven.

"Some investors may now see a greater chance of stronger US backing for Europe, easing the pressure on European governments to spend more [but] even with stronger US backing, we expect Europe to stick to its spending plans," she said.

"It takes years of investment to close the gaps in Europe's own military capabilities and build the industrial capacity it needs to deter Russia."

XRP sellers face massive slip as USD order book depth fakes size

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

At the Nov. 4 close, a capped sample of Bitstamp-issued USD offers held less funded inventory than the smaller RLUSD book.

An XRP Ledger order book sample advertised 1.28 million Bitstamp-issued USD tokens at the Nov. 4 close, but the offer owners' balances funded only about 57,000.

For an XRP holder selling into those offers, the headline quantity overstated the token inventory available to trade.

The smaller sample for dollar stablecoin Ripple USD (RLUSD) held about 92,000 funded tokens, with no gap between quoted and funded amounts at that ledger.

A Nov. 5 refresh, at ledger 108132129, closed at 13:09 UTC, still showed a large Bitstamp-dollar gap of roughly 1.31 million tokens quoted against 58,179 funded. RLUSD had about 80,435 quoted and 75,726 funded, so its earlier fully funded reading had not persisted.

The Nov. 4 reading covered validated ledger 108120236, closed at 23:59:51 UTC. Bitstamp-issued USD offers totaled 1,280,737 tokens quoted and 56,887 funded, a funded share of 4.44%.

One offer advertised 1,241,760 USD tokens while its funded amount was only 17,967. That single offer accounted for almost the entire difference, showing why adding advertised order sizes can mislead about what a counterparty can deliver.

The measurement requests at most 100 offers per side and counts quantities within 10% of each book's own reference midpoint, derived from its tightest uncrossed bid and ask. NOSHASHI, the ledger-data service, calls the dollar-token side that XRP sellers receive the “ask.”

XRPL's offer rules allow an owner to advertise more than it currently holds because placing an offer does not lock its full amount. The official book offers interface normally omits unfunded offers and identifies partially funded amounts, so software can distinguish advertised size from supported size.

An oversized Bitstamp USD offer drove the November 4 gap. Compare both book samples at the next day's measured ledger.

A larger advertised book can contain less funded token inventory for XRP sellers.

Bitstamp's IOU explanation describes redemption through the issuing platform and an account there. A holder's access to US dollars' redemption still depends on the applicable account and issuer conditions.

RLUSD's user terms name Standard Custody & Trust Company LLC as issuer, so direct redemption with Ripple requires being an eligible Customer under a separate agreement. Receiving either token on-ledger differs from receiving spendable dollars in a bank account.

Using the Nov. 5 ledger, a static model swept funded offers in price order for a 10,000-XRP sale. A separate model used each automated market maker (AMM), which trades against pool reserves, applying its ordinary trading fee in a constant-product calculation.

The book estimates use the 100-offer cap and 10% band. Pool estimates include ordinary AMM trading fees, and all four exclude additional issuer-transfer, network, and redemption costs, as well as wallet and trust-line constraints.

In the same model, a 50,000-XRP sale exhausted the sampled Bitstamp-dollar offers after about 47,484 XRP, returning 58,179 USD tokens and leaving 2,516 XRP unsold. The sampled RLUSD offers absorbed the modeled 50,000 XRP for about 64,960 RLUSD.

The RLUSD AMM held about 1.83 million XRP and 2.39 million RLUSD, versus only about 3,489 XRP and 4,594 USD tokens in the Bitstamp-dollar pool.

Quoted size, funded inventory, and completed trading volume answer different questions. For a seller, the useful test is a refreshed estimate for the intended size and acceptable issuer token, including the costs and redemption access needed to turn that token into cash.

Flaminio, Lazio e Soprintendenza verso l'intesa sulla pensilina

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Per la capienza si ragiona su 47-48mila posti, meno dei 50.570 previsti dalla proposta iniziale del club.

Il progetto della Lazio per lo stadio Flaminio procede, anche se lentamente: sarebbe stata trovata un’intesa con la Soprintendenza per conservare la pensilina originaria. Sul fronte della capienza, le parti sembrano essersi avvicinate a una soluzione tra 47mila e 48mila posti, mentre il club proverà a raggiungere i 50.570 posti previsti nella proposta iniziale. Il confronto va avanti attraverso compromessi, ma restano resistenze da superare.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Il numero di spettatori è uno dei temi principali del confronto tra la Lazio e la Soprintendenza. Da giorni sembra essere stata raggiunta un’intesa su una capienza compresa tra 47mila e 48mila posti: una cifra inferiore ai 50.570 prospettati inizialmente dal club biancoceleste. La Lazio continuerà a puntare a quell’obiettivo, ma non è scontato che riesca a ottenerlo.

Per arrivare alla capienza immaginata nella proposta di partenza dovranno essere superate le resistenze ancora presenti. L’accordo che sembra delinearsi sui 47mila-48mila posti rappresenta dunque un avvicinamento tra le parti, senza garantire che il numero possa salire fino a quello richiesto dalla Lazio. Il dialogo prosegue insieme agli altri passaggi necessari per far avanzare il progetto.

Un altro passo avanti riguarderebbe la pensilina originaria: la soluzione sulla quale sarebbe stato trovato un accordo ne prevede la conservazione. È uno degli elementi originali più riconoscibili e peculiari del progetto di Nervi, pensato per le Olimpiadi del 1960. Preservarla era un nodo cruciale per proseguire l’iter, soprattutto per il rapporto con l’inserimento del secondo anello e della nuova copertura.

La soluzione condivisa permetterebbe di mantenere il progetto originale e, allo stesso tempo, di far convivere la vecchia struttura con quella moderna. La tutela della pensilina si affianca così al confronto sulla capienza: sono i due temi sui quali il dialogo tra Lazio e Soprintendenza sembra aver prodotto passi avanti, pur senza chiudere tutte le questioni legate al progetto.

Leicester City confirm departure of Barnsley FC icon Conor Hourihane as ex-Leeds United man assumes new role

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Barnsley icon Conor Hourihane has left Leicester City less than five months on from his appointment as assistant manager.

The 35-year-old was a Reds hero in his playing days and made 136 appearances in the club’s colours. He later took the reins as the club’s head coach, but left at the end of last season.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

He bounced back into coaching with Leicester, taking up a role alongside Russell Martin.

However, just months into his spell as Martin’s number two, Hourihane has left the third-tier Foxes. Martin will now have coaching support from ex-Leeds United forward Steve Morison, who will combine coaching duties with his responsibilities as loans manager.

A club spokesperson said: “We can confirm that Leicester City assistant manager Conor Hourihane has left the football club. We thank Conor for his contribution during his time with us and wish him well for the future.

“First-team manager Russell Martin will continue to be supported by the existing coaching staff of Andy King, Tom Weal and Rhys Owen.

“Steve Morison will also provide coaching support to the first team alongside his existing responsibilities as loans manager.”

Hourihane has taken to Instagram to address his Leicester exit, insisting he is a “better coach” for his time with the club.

He said: “Wishing everyone at Leicester City all the very best moving forward, it’s a brilliant club with brilliant people. Really enjoyed my time and learned so much to make me a better coach going into my next role.”

Fans were left to wonder why Hourihane had left so early into his Foxes stay, but head coach Russell Martin has quelled speculation with a simple explanation.

He said: “I think purely down to a really honest conversation between me and Conor. He really wants to be a manager again. I'm really grateful he joined as a coach and wanted to learn.

“There's been opportunities that have come up here and abroad that maybe would have interested him if he'd been out of work. So we have to recognise that we want people here that are all in. It frees him up to take an opportunity.”

Retired midfielder Hourihane, who also had a loan spell at Sheffield United during his playing career, initially joined Barnsley in 2014. He was an influential figure during an exciting chapter in the club’s history, helping the Reds climb from League One into the Championship.

He left in 2017 before returning seven years later, taking up a player-coach role during the reign of Darrell Clarke. Hourihane hung up his boots shortly into his second Oakwell stay, focusing solely on his coaching duties.

He was handed the main role on a full-time basis when Clarke was dispensed of, but could not push the Reds into the League One promotion picture.

Ocarina Of Time Remake Devs Didn't Focus On The Zelda Timeline

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

For as long as The Legend of Zelda has existed, its timeline has caused confusion. Online debates have often raged over which games fall where on the timeline, and which timelines they even exist in to begin with.

With such a young development team, you'd expect The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time's Switch 2 remake to face a lot of timeline-related questions, especially since some developers admitted they hadn't played the original game. Zelda series producer Eiji Aonuma, who oversaw the remake, sought to simplify things with one piece of advice: just "focus on the game."

Spoilers for The Legend of Zelda timelines ahead.

The Legend of Zelda is split into multiple timelines. The Creation and Early Eras explores how Hyrule came to be and includes games like Skyward Sword and Ocarina of Time. Ocarina of Time then serves to split the series into three further timelines:

Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom then unite these timelines, telling the story of a distant legend. It's all a little bit messy, and with a fledgling development team, Aonuma revealed to Game Informer that he received lots of questions about it during development.

"It's funny because the staff would sometimes ask me about the possibility of including nods to the timeline or references to the other game," he said. "And I actually, at the time, would say, 'No, let's focus on this game. Let's make this the best version of Ocarina of Time that we can.' So, the timeline was in the back of our mind, but we didn't consider taking an approach to this game where we actually, explicitly included references like that."

No, let's focus on this game. Let's make this the best version of Ocarina of Time that we can.

Naturally, when you're developing a Zelda game, the timeline has to be considered, but as a remake, this makes total sense. The new Ocarina of Time team was redoing a self-contained story, and one where the timeline had already been determined. They didn't need to focus on the cause and effect, just making the best version of the game possible, and they seemed to do just that.

It's now comfortably the best-reviewed game of 2026, at least until we find out what GTA 6 scores, and it received a 5/5 from us here at TheGamer. In our review, we said, "Nintendo’s remake feels like a triumphant celebration of what made it so special while wisely modernizing small aspects to make for a more enjoyable experience," praising the new art style, auditory updates, and gameplay balance.

Ajman Government Media Office unveils vision and programme for inaugural Ajman Media Forum 2026

An interactive programme featuring media innovation challenges, emerging technologies and data-driven dialogue

AJMAN, UAE, Nov. 6, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- The Ajman Government Media Office unveiled the strategic vision, objectives, and key themes of the inaugural Ajman Media Forum 2026 â THE SIGNAL, during a press conference at Thara Centre for Entrepreneurship in Ajman. Scheduled to take place on 18 and 19 November under the theme 'Who Owns the Signal?', the Forum will bring together strategic partners and feature a programme combining thought-provoking dialogue, practical experiences and technological innovation.

The Forum forms part of Ajman's efforts to strengthen its position as a regional hub for media dialogue and innovation, bringing together local and international expertise across the media, technology, creative economy and scientific research sectors. It aims to explore the transformations reshaping the industry while advancing trust, credibility and innovation in an increasingly dynamic media landscape.

HE Mohammed Abdullah Al Kaabi, Executive Director of the Ajman Government Media Office, said: "The launch of the Forum comes amid profound transformations across the media landscape, as the ability to shape narratives and influence public opinion increasingly extends beyond traditional media institutions and channels. Digital platforms, algorithms, artificial intelligence and content creators are playing a growing role in determining how information is produced, distributed and consumed."

He added: "Against this backdrop, the Forum takes 'Who Owns the Signal?' as its central question, opening a multidisciplinary dialogue on the changing dynamics of influence, the future of trust and credibility, and the responsibility of media and government institutions in shaping a more innovative and reliable media environment."

HE Al Kaabi explained that the Forum's programme is structured around five key themes: media transformations and the future of the industry; artificial intelligence in content creation; building trust and countering disinformation; national reputation and government communication; and digital platforms and the media economy. Together, these themes reflect an integrated approach to media that brings together technology, economics, communication, culture and social responsibility.

He affirmed that the Forum is supported by a network of strategic partners, including the National Media Authority, TRENDS Group, the Department of Economic Development â Ajman, Abu Dhabi Media Network, Arabian Radio Network (ARN), the Municipality and Planning Department â Ajman, and the Transport Authority in Ajman. Each partner will contribute its expertise and capabilities to enriching the programme and expanding its impact.

The partnership with the National Media Authority will support the development of a platform that brings together expertise, empowers national talent and creates new opportunities for collaboration and innovation.

As part of efforts to advance the creative economy, the Department of Economic Development â Ajman will participate as the Creative Economy Development Partner, with a dedicated pavilion showcasing UAE-based creative media companies. The pavilion will enable participating companies to present their work and solutions, build professional connections, and explore opportunities for collaboration and investment.

TRENDS Group will participate through a dedicated platform highlighting the capabilities of its five companies across scientific research, strategic consultancy, training, artificial intelligence and future foresight. The Group will also contribute to a keynote session, offering research-driven perspectives on the transformations reshaping the media sector. The Municipality and Planning Department â Ajman will participate as the Quality of Life and Urban Sustainability Sponsor, highlighting media's role in showcasing development projects and raising community awareness. The Department will present its AM30x30 agenda and related initiatives and projects to support Ajman's urban development and enhance quality of life.

Meanwhile, the Transport Authority in Ajman, as the Official Transport Partner, will facilitate transportation for VIPs and Forum guests, support the promotional campaign across its transport channels, and host a dedicated pavilion showcasing its services and initiatives.

Abu Dhabi Media Network will help expand the forum's reach and bring its discussions and insights to wider audiences through its television, print and digital platforms. Meanwhile, Arabian Radio Network (ARN) will strengthen the forum's presence across radio and audio platforms, engaging listeners and extending the conversation to new audiences.

Beyond its panel discussions and presentations, the forum will offer a series of interactive experiences, including the Mediathon, which will bring participants together to tackle real-world media challenges and develop practical ideas, projects and solutions through innovation, collaboration and hands-on learning.

The forum will also host a roundtable bringing together media leaders and decision-makers to examine the major shifts reshaping the industry. Drawing on data, research and global indicators, and supported by specialised knowledge partners, including Statista, the discussions will seek to generate actionable recommendations that address emerging challenges, anticipate future developments and help shape the sector's priorities.

The 'AI Media Lab' will offer visitors a hands-on opportunity to explore the technologies transforming the media landscape. Through live demonstrations of AI-powered content generation, news verification and other emerging applications, participants will be able to test these tools, understand their capabilities and explore their potential uses across the industry.

Complementing these experiences, 'Ajman Studios' will provide dedicated spaces for discussions and interviews with media leaders, industry experts and influential content creators. The studios will also support the production of original content, capturing key insights and perspectives from the forum and sharing them with audiences across multiple platforms, extending the impact of its discussions beyond the two-day event.

The forum will further expand its international dimension by spotlighting China's advances in media and technology innovation. Specialised companies and institutions will showcase their expertise, technologies and practical applications in artificial intelligence, media and content creation, creating opportunities for knowledge exchange, collaboration and stronger ties between the UAE and China.

By bringing together strategic partnerships, industry dialogue and hands-on experiences, Ajman Media Forum 2026 aims to create a platform where ideas translate into practical outcomes. Through this approach, the forum seeks to advance understanding of the forces shaping the media industry, encourage the development of innovative solutions and reinforce Ajman's position as a centre for regional and international dialogue on the future of media.

Hyperliquid Strategies seeks futures registration for perps

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The HYPE treasury company spent $785 million on tokens during the quarter and wants to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the US.

A subsidiary of Nasdaq-listed HYPE treasury company Hyperliquid Strategies has applied to become a futures commission merchant (FCM), seeking to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to the US.

“Owning HYPE is how we aim to capture economics associated with the Hyperliquid protocol; setting up an FCM is how we intend to help bring markets deployed on Hyperliquid onshore,” said Hyperliquid Strategies CEO David Schamis in the company’s results for the quarter ended Sept. 30, published on Thursday.

Hyperliquid’s HIP-3 framework allows independent operators to launch custom perpetual futures markets on the protocol’s onchain infrastructure by staking 500,000 HYPE tokens ($38.8 million). Proposed HIP-3 features would allow operators to create permissioned markets that restrict participation to approved addresses.

The subsidiary would handle US customer accounts for permissioned markets deployed on Hyperliquid, the company said. Both the FCM application and the proposed markets require regulatory approval.

Intercontinental Exchange, the parent company of the New York Stock Exchange, announced an agreement with Trade[XYZ] on Oct. 29 to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual markets to the US, subject to regulatory approvals. ICE plans to operate a dedicated market and clearing structure.

Related: Hyperliquid, Pump.fun account for nearly 90% of record $638M crypto buybacks: FT

During the quarter, Hyperliquid Strategies raised $911 million in equity capital. It spent $785 million to acquire 9.1 million HYPE and $17 million to repurchase 2.7 million PURR shares. The company held a total of 38.5 million HYPE as of Sept. 30, valued at $3.5 billion at the time.

“Hyperliquid is winning the parts of onchain finance that generate real fees, and nearly all of that value flows back to HYPE holders through programmatic buybacks,” Schamis said.

Hyperliquid Strategies’ shares fell 9.6% Thursday, closing at $8.76, according to Yahoo Finance.

Broadcom Stock Has Had Only 2 Losing Years Since 2009. 2026 Is on Pace to Be the 3rd.

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After the last two, the stock rose 24% and then almost doubled. Can it rebound like that again?

2026 hasn't been good to Broadcom (AVGO -10.43%) stock. At roughly $300 as I write this, the chip designer's shares trade about 13% under their 2025 close of $346.10.

A losing year is rare for this stock. Since its August 2009 listing as Avago Technologies, the stock has ended just two calendar years lower. Shares dipped around 1% in 2018 and dropped 16% in 2022. But both times, a strong year followed, with the stock gaining 24% in 2019 and almost doubling in 2023.

So it's tempting to read this year's drop as the setup for another rebound. I'm not sure the pattern holds this time, though. The S&P 500 (^GSPC -4.36%) dropped around 6% in 2018 and 19% in 2022, so Broadcom beat the index both times. This year, the index is up about 9%.

If 2026 ended today, it'd be the first calendar year since its debut that Broadcom lagged the S&P 500 by over 5 percentage points.

Neither earlier drop was about the business. Broadcom's revenue climbed 18% in fiscal 2018 to almost $21 billion. Free cash flow grew even faster, up 50%. Fiscal 2022 revenue rose 21% to a record $33.2 billion.

Strangely, the rebound years were the slower ones. In June 2019, Broadcom cut its fiscal 2019 revenue forecast to $22.5 billion from $24.5 billion, as CEO Hock Tan pointed to "a broad-based slowdown in the demand environment." Revenue finished the year up 8%. And the stock's 24% rise in 2019 lagged the S&P 500's 29%, so that rebound mainly rode the market.

Fiscal 2023 revenue also rose just 8%. But Tan said that growth came from "investments in accelerators and network connectivity for AI by hyperscalers," and Broadcom forecast around $50 billion in fiscal 2024 revenue with VMware included, up 40%.

The starting price probably helped in both cases. Shares closed 2018 at around 12 times earnings and 2022 at about 15 times earnings, using non-GAAP (adjusted) earnings per share for the fiscal year just ended.

Broadcom's business is growing faster today than in any of those years. Revenue jumped 86% year over year to $29.6 billion in the fiscal third quarter of 2026 (the period ending Aug. 2, 2026), led by artificial intelligence (AI) semiconductor revenue of $16.7 billion, up 221%. Net income climbed 216%. And free cash flow has grown for three straight quarters, from $7.5 billion in fiscal Q4 2025 to $13.7 billion in fiscal Q3.

But shares have shed more than a third of their value since closing at a record $481.57 on June 2.

The stock also doesn't start at a cheap price. Shares trade at around 30 times adjusted earnings for the past four reported quarters, at least twice either earlier starting point. On fiscal 2027's expected earnings, though, the price-to-earnings ratio is about 16, which arguably looks fair for a business growing so fast.

Broadcom has said it plans to release its fiscal fourth-quarter results on Wednesday, Dec. 9.

Management guided for fiscal fourth-quarter revenue of about $34.8 billion, up 93% year over year, with $21.7 billion in AI semiconductor revenue.

The bigger test is fiscal 2027, when Broadcom expects AI semiconductor revenue to double to around $115 billion.

"This AI revenue guidance is being provided to you to give you the trajectory of our growth, and we do not intend to update it on a quarterly basis," CFO Amie Thuener said on the Sept. 2 earnings call.

So December's guidance for the fiscal first quarter of 2027 might be the closest check investors get. Hitting $115 billion takes an average of almost $29 billion in AI revenue a quarter, well over the $21.7 billion guided for the fiscal fourth quarter. The December report has also usually brought the annual dividend increase, which was 10% last year.

When I wrote in late September that Broadcom had climbed in 13 straight fourth quarters, shares were around $350. A gain of about 17% from here would continue that streak, and about 15% would keep 2026 out of the loss column.

Ultimately, Broadcom's business looks far stronger now than it did in 2018 or 2022. But this year's drop is Broadcom's own, not the market's, and shares don't start out cheap. Sure, a soft first-quarter outlook may send the stock down before the year ends.

I still think the stock is worth a look at this price. I'd just want to see that outlook before counting on another year like 2023.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Broadcom. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

CytomX (CTMX) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Lower diarrhea rates emerge, but toxicity control remains the key test.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

CytomX Therapeutics, Inc. (CTMX -8.94%) reported third quarter financial results and outlined a strategic transition toward late-stage clinical development for its lead program, Varseta-M. Management emphasized the first-in-class status of the EpCAM-directed antibody-drug conjugate, noting that its proprietary PROBODY platform enables the targeting of antigens previously considered undruggable. The company is advancing Varseta-M toward its first potential registrational study in metastatic colorectal cancer during the first half of 2027, while simultaneously initiating studies in earlier-line combination therapies and other gastrointestinal malignancies. Furthermore, the company maintains a cash runway through late 2028, supported by its collaboration with Regeneron and disciplined capital allocation toward its most compelling clinical priorities.

Operator: Good afternoon, everyone. Thank you for standing by. Welcome to the CytomX Therapeutics third quarter 26 financial results call. Please be advised that today's call is being recorded. I would now like to hand the call over to your host for today, Christopher W. Ogden, CytomX's chief financial officer. Please go ahead.

Christopher W. Ogden: Thank you. Good afternoon, and thank you for joining us. Before we begin, I would like to remind everyone that during this call, we will be making forward-looking statements. Important risks and uncertainties are set forth in our most recent public filings with the SEC, at sec.gov. Except as required by law, we undertake no obligation to update any forward-looking whether as a result of new information, future developments or otherwise.

Earlier this afternoon, we filed our quarterly report on Form 10 Q for Q3 26 and issued a press release with a summary of our third quarter 26 financial results and business highlights. We encourage everyone to read today's filing and press release. Additionally, the press release recording of this call and our SEC filings can be found under the Investors and News section of our website. With me on the call today is Dr. Sean McCarthy, CytomX Chief Executive Officer and Chairman. Sean will provide an update on our pipeline and company progress, before I cover the financials for the quarter. We will then conclude with a Q&A session. With that, I will turn the call over to Sean.

Sean A. McCarthy DPHIL: Thanks, Christopher, and good afternoon, everyone. We are very pleased to be here today to provide an update on our third quarter developments and what continues to be a highly productive year for CytomX as we advance our lead program, Varseta-M, towards its first potential registrational study in the first half of 27. Varseta-M is an EpCAM targeting topo-1 antibody-drug conjugate with strong potential in colorectal cancer and potentially other solid tumors. Importantly, to our knowledge, Varseta-M is first in class as the only EpCAM directed ADC in clinical development. Viceta-M has been intentionally designed and uniquely enabled by our PROBODY platform, creating a significant opportunity for differentiation and long term value creation.

Strategically, we are initially developing Varseta-M in metastatic colorectal cancer as the lead indication. Our sense of urgency for Varseta-M is driven by the significant unmet need in colorectal cancer which is the second leading cause of cancer death in the United States and worldwide. Varseta-M is on the leading edge of ADC innovation in CRC and has the potential to be 1 of the first monotherapy ADCs to market for the treatment of this very challenging cancer type. In terms of commercial potential, there are an estimated 35 thousand addressable patients in the United States with third-line or later CRC.

Given the anticancer activity we have reported previously, across an unselected late stage patient population and the limited clinical benefit of existing therapies for these patients, we see considerable opportunity for Varseta-M. Based on Varseta-M's unique profile, we are actively advancing our development program across 3 strategic areas of priority. First, we aim to move Varseta-M monotherapy into a registrational study in the first half of next year enabling a potential first approval and meaningful market opportunity in metastatic CRC. Second, we continue our development of Varseta-M in combinations, including with bevacizumab and with earlier line chemotherapy regimens with the goal of replacing current standard of care.

And third, we have started to evaluate Varseta-M's pan tumor potential across multiple EpCAM expressing cancers initially in additional gastrointestinal indications. The monotherapy for Varseta-M Phase 1 study has enrolled more than 100 patients across dose-escalation, expansion, and optimization cohorts. As previously reported, patients enrolled in the phase 1 study have been heavily pretreated with most patients having received 3 or more prior treatments in the metastatic setting. In March, we reported encouraging progression-free survival at doses of 8.6 and 10 milligrams per kilogram that exceeded current standard of care. We also shared our increasing understanding of safety profile of Varseta-M, particularly how to lower and manage rates of high grade diarrhea.

This initial experience with Varceta m in very late-line patients will provide us with a highly relevant dataset to enable monotherapy dose selection and inform the potential first registrational study design. Most recently, we have been highly focused on dose optimization, evaluating 2 doses, 8.6 and 10 milligrams per kilogram given every 3 weeks with adjusted ideal body weight dosing and upfront prophylaxis to manage gastrointestinal adverse events.

We anticipate providing an integrated update in January across the phase 1 study, including our potential registrational strategy, and the data that underpin our plans. it is important to note here that we expect this next update to provide the first look at overall survival for Varseta-M which we anticipate will be the primary endpoint in our initial registrational study. We look forward to providing this next update in the near term. Now moving on to the Varseta-M combination development program. Our ongoing phase 1 study evaluating Varseta-M in combination with bevacizumab continues to enroll late-line CRC patients to assess dose and safety. We view this combination as a foundational first step in evaluating Varseta-M's potential in earlier-line CRC.

Subject to data from the ongoing phase 1 study we anticipate moving to earlier line CRC patients in the future evaluation of this combination and we anticipate reporting initial clinical data from the bevacizumab combination in the first half of 27. To also enable the use of Varseta-M in earlier lines of CRC therapy, we remain on track to initiate a study evaluating Varseta-M in combination with bevacizumab, 5-fluorouracil and leucovorin. By the end of the year. This study will be a new Phase 1/2 trial that would enroll second line CRC patients once phase 1 dose finding is successfully completed.

We believe these Varceta m combination studies have the potential to inform future development options and look forward to providing initial updates starting next year. Now moving to the development of Varseta-M outside of CRC, our strategy is to develop and ultimately commercialize Varseta-M across multiple EpCAM expressing solid tumors. And we have recently initiated phase 1 cohorts in gastrointestinal indications with substantial unmet clinical need. and that we view as highly complementary to CRC from a commercial perspective. Have initiated cohorts in unselected gastric and gastroesophageal junction cancer, as well as EpCAM selected pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma and EPCAM selected biliary tract cancer.

Each of these EpCAM expressing tumor types currently have limited treatment options in later-line settings, and may present expedited development pathways for Varseta-M upon clinical proof-of-concept. Our initial plan is to enroll approximately 20 patients per tumor type to evaluate Varseta-M's antitumor activity and safety before assessing later phase development options. Having focused for Varseta-M exclusively in CRC for the first 2 years of development, we are very excited to now be evaluating Varseta-M's broader clinical potential. Now turning to CX-801, our masked interferon alpha 2 b program, being evaluated in checkpoint refractory melanoma.

We see CX-801 as an exciting and broad to reprogram the immune responsiveness of solid tumors and we continue to enroll the ongoing phase 1 study evaluating CX-801 as a monotherapy and in combination with KEYTRUDA. Enrollment in the monotherapy cohort of our phase 1 study continues. Having exceeded dose levels of the approved dose of unmasked interferon alpha 2 b. Initial biomarker data for CX-801 monotherapy presented previously have reinforced our mechanism of action hypothesis for CX-801 in combination with KEYTRUDA. We continue to enroll in the combination cohort, and we look forward to sharing initial clinical data from the phase 1 study in melanoma in the first half of 27.

With those updates, let me now transition the call back to Christopher.

Christopher W. Ogden: Thank you, Sean. As Sean highlighted, we are making important progress across the Varseta-M program. Including potentially moving towards later stage development. As we progress the broadening Varseta-M development program, we have continued to maintain an efficient and flexible cost structure enabling us to pursue the opportunities Sean described earlier. To that end, with our current balance sheet, we project cash runway into the second half of 28. Importantly, our runway funds our key value creation priorities that Sean outlined. Including advancing Varseta-M into its first potential registrational study pursuing combination development to move Varseta-M into earlier lines of CRC, and then expanding into multiple other EpCAM expressing gastrointestinal tumors.

Workiva (WK) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Agent Studio launch and a big bank expansion highlight the quarter.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Workiva Inc. (WK +0.26%) reported results for the third quarter of 2026, highlighted by growth in large customer contracts and the launch of new artificial intelligence capabilities. Management stated that the company is transitioning from enabling regulatory work to executing it through agentic solutions. The company reported continued expansion in its large customer cohorts and maintained its full-year guidance for revenue and cash flow while raising its non-GAAP operating margin outlook to a range of 18.2% to 18.3%. Management attributed this performance to steady execution across its solution portfolio and a growing multi-solution footprint, with 77% of subscription revenue now derived from customers using multiple products.

Our ongoing focus on operational discipline continues to be reflected in our expanding margins. Non-GAAP operating margin for our third quarter reached 18.3%. In Q3, contracts valued over $300,000 annually grew 31%, and contracts above $500,000 annually grew 20%, both compared to Q3 of 2025. Year-to-date, our performance reflects the durability of our business, our expanding value proposition, and the steady momentum across our customer base and our Partner Ecosystem. In September, we welcomed our customers, partners, analysts, and investors to Las Vegas for our annual Amplify conference and 2026 Investor Day.

One message came through clearly: AI is fundamentally changing enterprise software, and it is raising the bar for what customers expect from Workiva. As AI takes on more complex, high-stakes regulatory work, trust becomes even more critical. Enterprises need confidence that AI-driven outputs are governed, traceable, and auditable. That shift plays directly to Workiva's strengths. It creates an opportunity for us to expand our strategic role, moving from enabling critical regulatory work to increasingly taking on more of that work for our customers. As AI becomes more central to the enterprise, our strategic relevance is showing up in our customer wins.

First, we launched Agent Studio, which enables customers and partners to quickly build, customize, and deploy AI agents directly within Workiva's trusted platform, automating sophisticated business processes without writing code. Second, we launched an Agentic Solution for automated testing that orchestrates evidence, attribute, and testing agents to automate manual workflows with full traceability.

This new GRC offering expands sampling, risk coverage, and testing capacity for our customers without additional manual effort. Barbara Larson: Thanks, Julie. I will begin by walking through our third quarter financial results and key metrics, then share our guidance for the fourth quarter and full year 2026. We saw steady execution across the business in Q3, with broad-based demand across our solution portfolio. Total revenue for the quarter was $263 million, up 17% year-over-year and exceeding the high end of our guidance by $1 million. Foreign currency fluctuations were a modest headwind to our reported top-line growth rate, in line with expectations. Looking at the composition of our revenue, subscription revenue totaled $246 million, up 17% year-over-year.
